Weighted Sobolev space theory for the heat equation and the time-fractional heat equation in non-smooth domains Thanks: The author was supported by a KIAS Individual Grant (MG095802) at Korea Institute for Advanced Study.
Jinsol Seo

Abstract
. We present a general L p L_{p} -solvability framework for both the classical and time-fractional heat equations in non-smooth domains under the zero Dirichlet boundary condition. We consider domains Ω \Omega admitting the Hardy inequality: There exists a constant N > 0 N>0 such that ∫ Ω | f ⁡ ( x ) d ⁡ ( x , ∂ Ω ) | 2 ​ 𝑑 x ≤ N ​ ∫ Ω | ∇ f | 2 ​ 𝑑 x for any f ∈ C c ∞ ​ ( Ω ) . \int_{\Omega}\Big|\frac{f(x)}{d(x,\partial\Omega)}\Big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq N\int_{\Omega}|\nabla f|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for any}\quad f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)\,. To illustrate the boundary behavior of solutions in a general framework, we employ a weight system composed of a superharmonic function and a distance function to the boundary. Further, we investigate applications to various non-smooth domains, including convex domains, domains with exterior cone condition, totally vanishing exterior Reifenberg domains, and domains Ω ⊂ ℝ d \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} for which the Aikawa dimension of Ω c \Omega^{c} is less than d − 2 d-2 . By using superharmonic functions tailored to the geometric conditions of the domain, we derive weighted L p L_{p} -solvability results for various non-smooth domains, with specific weight ranges that differ for each domain condition. In addition, we provide an application to the Hölder continuity of solutions in domains with the volume density condition, as well as pointwise estimates for solutions in Lipschitz cones.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35K05; 35R11, 42B35, 31B05, 26D10

1.
Introduction

The heat equation is one of the most classical partial differential equations.
Historically,
L_{p}
-theory for this equation in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
C^{2}
-domains was developed in parallel with Schauder- and
L_{2}
-theories.
Extensions have been extensively developed in various directions, including variable coefficients
[
43
]
, semigroups
[
24
,
51
]
, and non-smooth domains.

In this paper, we develop a general
L_{p}
-theory for the heat equation in
non-smooth
domains
\Omega
, under the zero-Dirichlet boundary condition:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\Delta u+f\quad\text{in}\,\,\,(0,\infty)\times\Omega\quad;\quad u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}\,\,,\,\,u|_{(0,\infty)\times\partial\Omega}\equiv 0\,.

(1.1)

Unweighted and weighted
L_{p}
-theories for (
1.1
) have been developed on various types of domains, such as
C^{1}
-domains
[
33
]
, Reifenberg domains
[
8
]
, convex domains and Lipschitz domains
[
69
]
, smooth cones
[
37
,
58
,
63
]
.

In addition to the classical heat equation, we also consider the following time-fractional heat equation of order
0<\alpha<1
within a unified framework:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=\Delta u+f

\displaystyle\quad\text{in}\,\,\,(0,\infty)\times\Omega\quad

\displaystyle;\quad u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}\,\,,\,\,u|_{(0,\infty)\times\partial\Omega}\equiv 0\,,

(1.2)

where
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}
is the Caputo fractional derivative (see Definition
2.1
).
In physical terms, equation (
1.2
) describes subdiffusion.
While normal diffusion, modeled by the classical heat equation, describes particle systems where the mean squared displacement is proportional to time
t
, subdiffusion characterizes systems in which the mean-squared displacement grows proportionally to
t^{\alpha}
.
This model has numerous important applications in probability and mechanics; for further discussion, we refer the reader to
[
55
,
56
,
57
]
.

The
L_{p}
-theory for (
1.2
) has been developed via various approaches, including a functional analytic and
H^{\infty}
calculus methods
[
59
,
71
]
, a kernel approach
[
21
,
22
,
30
]
, and a kernel-free approach
[
13
,
15
]
.
Regarding the domain
\Omega
appearing in (
1.2
), these studies mainly consider the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as well as
C^{2}
- and
C^{1}
-domains.
Although many other relevant references are available, we omit a detailed discussion to focus on our primary interest.

In
[
62
]
, the present author developed a general weighted
L_{p}
-theory for the Poisson equation in non-smooth domains.
As a natural continuation of that work, we extend this framework and establish a weighted
L_{p}
-theory for (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) in non-smooth domains.
We further develop the framework of
[
62
]
, obtaining nontrivial results for time-measurable coefficients (see Sections
5.3
and
5.4
) and establishing pointwise estimates for homogeneous solutions on Lipschitz cones (see Section
5.5
).

We consider domains
\Omega\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{d}
admitting the Hardy inequality: There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\Big|\frac{f(x)}{d(x,\partial\Omega)}\Big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)\int_{\Omega}|\nabla f(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all}\quad f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)\,.

(1.3)

A sufficient geometric condition ensuring (
1.3
) is the volume density condition:

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\partial\Omega\\
r>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\big|\Omega^{c}\cap B_{r}(p)\big|}{\big|B_{r}(p)\big|}>0

(1.4)

(see Proposition
5.7
and (
5.7
)).
We establish that for equations (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) with a domain
\Omega
admitting (
1.3
), each
superharmonic Harnack function

\psi

immediately leads to a weighted
L_{p}
-solvability result associated with
\psi
, for general
p\in(1,\infty)
(see (
1.5
)).
Here, a superharmonic Harnack function is a locally integrable function
\psi
that satisfies the following conditions:

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\text{$\Delta\psi\leq 0$ in the sense of distributions, and}\\
\text{$\sup_{B(x,\rho(x)/2)}\psi\lesssim\inf_{B(x,\rho(x)/2)}\psi$ for all $x\in\Omega$,}\end{gathered}

where
\rho(x):=d(x,\partial\Omega)
.
In our results,
\psi
describes the boundary behavior of solutions.
We apply our result to various types of non-smooth domains through appropriate superharmonic functions.
We discuss our main results and their applications in detail in Section
1.2
.

1.1.
Historical remarks for the classical heat equation.

In studying the theory of the heat equation in non-smooth domains, it is instructive to recall previous works on the Poisson equation with zero Dirichlet boundary conditions.
Notable contributions include those by Jerison and Kenig
[
25
]
in Lipschitz domains, Adolfsson
[
1
]
and Fromm
[
17
]
in convex domains, studies in smooth cones and polyhedrons presented in the monographs
[
6
,
53
,
54
]
, and Byun and Wang
[
7
]
in Reifenberg domains.
We refer the reader to
[
62
]
for further discussion of these works, and next focus on the heat equation (
1.1
).

The extension of the results for the Poisson equation to the heat equation in Lipschitz and convex domains was established by Wood
[
69
, Theorem 6.1]
, which provides a universal range of
p
for the unweighted
L_{p}
-theory.
However, the impossibility of establishing a general unweighted
L_{p}
-theory for all
1<p<\infty
for the Poisson equation, as shown in
[
25
, Theorem A]
(see also
[
69
, Theorem 7.1]
), also extends to the heat equation.
Given these limitations in unweighted Sobolev spaces, it is natural to turn to theories in weighted Sobolev spaces.

There are several works on the
L_{p}
-theory for the heat equation in smooth cones, including those by Solonnikov
[
63
]
, Nazarov
[
58
]
, and Kozlov and Nazarov
[
37
]
.
Here, a smooth cone is defined by
\Omega:=\{r\sigma\,:\,r>0\,,\,\,\sigma\in\mathcal{M}\}
, where
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth subdomain of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
For such domains, scholars have investigated the unique solvability of the heat equation in specific types of weighted
L_{p}
-Sobolev spaces for general
p\in(1,\infty)
.
The weight system in these spaces is based on the distance function from the vertex.
The admissible range of weights ensuring unique solvability is closely related to the
eigenvalues
of the spherical Laplacian on
\mathcal{M}
.

These studies suggest that developing a unified framework for the
L_{p}
-solvability of the heat equation in general non-smooth domains requires adopting a weight system associated with the Laplace operator and the geometric features of each domain.

In the context of the weighted
L_{p}
-theory, we focus on the localization argument developed by Krylov
[
40
]
, which was originally devised to establish the
L_{p}
-theory of the stochastic heat equation in smooth domains (see
[
31
,
38
]
).
Krylov’s work focuses on the heat equation in half-spaces and was later extended to
C^{1}
-domains by Kim and Krylov
[
33
]
.
Although these works were originally confined to half-spaces and
C^{1}
domains, the localization argument was later extended to non-smooth domains, as demonstrated by
[
32
,
34
]
.
In the study of smooth cones
[
34
]
, the argument was adapted to a broader weight system and combined with heat kernel estimates.
Additionally, Kim
[
32
]
established a connection between Krylov’s approach
[
40
]
and the classical Hardy inequality (
1.3
).

However, the approaches in
[
32
,
34
]
have some limitations.
The method in
[
34
]
relies on sharp kernel estimates, and the heat kernels have only been investigated for a few classes of domains.
In
[
32
]
, the range of weights requires further refinement to adequately capture the boundary behavior of solutions.
In particular, the results in
[
32
]
do not include the cases of the half-space
[
40
]
and of
C^{1}
domains
[
31
]
.

1.2.
Main result and its applications to various domain conditions

In line with
[
32
]
, we restrict our attention to the class of domains admitting the Hardy inequality.
This choice is motivated by the observation that the Hardy inequality holds on various non-smooth domains (see (
1.4
)).
A key distinguishing feature of the present paper, compared with earlier studies, is the utilization of superharmonic functions in conjunction with the Hardy inequality.
This combination enables us to accurately describe the boundary behavior of solutions.
Our main result, Theorem
4.8
, establishes the following estimate together with a related solvability result:

Let
\Omega
admit the Hardy inequality (
1.3
) and
\psi
be a superharmonic Harnack function in
\Omega
.
For any
1<p<\infty
and
-\frac{1}{p}<\mu<1-\frac{1}{p}
, if
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}((0,\infty)\times\Omega)
and
f_{0},\,f_{1},\,\ldots,\,f_{d}
satisfy
\partial_{t}u=\Delta u+f_{0}+\sum_{i\geq 1}D_{i}f_{i}
, then we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\psi^{-\mu}\rho^{-2/p}u\|_{p}+\|\psi^{-\mu}\rho^{-2/p+1}D_{x}u\|_{p}\\
\lesssim\,&\|\psi^{-\mu}\rho^{-2/p+2}f_{0}\|_{p}+\sum_{i\geq 1}\|\psi^{-\mu}\rho^{-2/p+1}f_{i}\|_{p}\,.\end{split}

(1.5)

Here,
\rho(x):=d(x,\partial\Omega)
.
In (
1.5
), the superharmonic Harnack function
\psi
represents the boundary behavior of solutions.
Through the Sobolev-Hölder embedding theorem, we further obtain pointwise estimates for solutions (see (
2.25
) and Proposition
4.7
).

Our main result does not specify the superharmonic Harnack function
\psi
.
The flexibility in selecting
\psi
is a crucial advantage of our theorem, which allows applications to a wide range of non-smooth domains
\Omega
.
Throughout Section
5
, we investigate applications to various geometric domain conditions.
Specifically, we apply superharmonic functions
\psi
such that
\psi\simeq d(\cdot,\partial\Omega)^{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, where the range of
\lambda
is different for each domain condition.
Additionally, in Lipschitz cones, we apply the weighted
L_{p}
-theory to obtain pointwise estimates for solutions.

We next outline applications of our main result to various domain conditions.
Consider a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 2
, and denote
\rho(x):=d(x,\partial\Omega)
.
For
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
n\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
, we define

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{n}(\Omega,T)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\|D_{x}^{k}f\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p,\theta+kp}(\Omega,T)}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\big|D_{x}^{k}f(t,x)\big|^{p}\rho(x)^{\theta+kp}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{1/p}\,,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{-n}(\Omega,T)}

\displaystyle:=\inf\bigg\{\sum_{|\beta|\leq n}\|f_{\beta}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p,\theta-|\beta|p}(\Omega,T)}\,:\,f=\sum_{|\beta|\leq n}D_{x}^{\beta}f_{\beta}\bigg\}\,.

\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{n}(\Omega,T)
denotes the set of all
f
such that
\|f\|_{\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{n}(\Omega,T)}<\infty
.

Remark 1.1
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the spaces
\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{n}(\Omega,T)
appear only in this subsection.
However, these spaces coincide with
\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+d}^{n}(\Omega,T)
(see (
2.10
) and (
2.28
) with putting
\Psi\equiv 1
).
Here,
\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+d}^{n}(\Omega,T)
denotes the function space introduced in (
4.1
).

Consider the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=\mathcal{L}u+f:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a^{ij}(t)D_{ij}u+f\quad\text{on }(0,T]\times\Omega\,,

(1.6)

where
0<\alpha\leq 1
and
0<T<\infty
.
Here,
\partial_{t}^{1}
coincides with the classical derivative, and for
0<\alpha<1
,
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}
is the Caputo derivative (see Definitions
2.1
and
4.1
).
In addition, the coefficients
a^{ij}(\cdot)
are measurable functions of
t
and satisfy the uniform ellipticity condition: there exists
0<\nu\leq 1
such that

\displaystyle\nu|\xi|^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a^{ij}(t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\nu^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,\,0<t\leq T\,,\,\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,.

(1.7)

We restrict our attention to both the zero initial data condition and the zero Dirichlet boundary condition,

\displaystyle u(t,x)=0\quad\text{if}\,\,\,\,t=0\,\,\,\,\text{or}\,\,\,\,x\in\partial\Omega\,.

(1.8)

The results in Sections
4
and
5
also cover the nonzero initial data problem, where the initial data lie in the space
B_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, as introduced in (
2.11
) and (
2.28
) (with
\Psi\equiv 1
).
For the rigorous definition of equation (
1.6
) with (
1.8
) or nonzero initial data, see Definition
4.1
.
For simplicity of exposition, we omit the treatment of this case in the present subsection.

1) Exterior cone condition.

For
\delta\in[0,\pi/2)
and
R>0
,
\Omega
is said to satisfy the exterior
(\delta,R)
-cone condition if for every
p\in\partial\Omega
, there exists a unit vector
e_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p}\geq|x-p|\cos\delta\,\,,\,\,|x-p|<R\}\subset\Omega^{c}\,.

When
\delta=0
, this condition is often called the exterior
R
-line segment condition.
An example of this condition is illustrated in Figure
5.2
.
Although our result is applicable to some unbounded domains, we restrict our attention here to bounded domains.

For given
\delta\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, let
\lambda_{\delta}
be the constant defined in (
5.4
).
When
d=2
, we set
\lambda_{0}=1/2
.
Note that
\lambda_{\delta}=\frac{\pi}{2(\pi-\delta)}
if
d=2
;
\lambda_{\delta}=\frac{\delta}{\pi-\delta}
if
d=4
; and
\lambda_{\delta}>0
for all
d\geq 2
and
\delta>0
.

Theorem 1.2

(see Corollary
5.9
and Example
5.6
.(3))
.

Let
\delta\in(0,\pi)
if
d\geq 3
, and
\delta\in[0,\pi)
if
d=2
.
Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded domain satisfying the
(\delta,R)
-exterior cone condition for some
0<R<\infty
.
Then for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying

\displaystyle-2-(p-1)\lambda_{\delta}<\theta-d<-2+\lambda_{\delta}\,,

if
f\in\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta+2p}^{n}(\Omega,T)
, then equation (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) with
\mathcal{L}:=\Delta
has a unique solution
u
in
\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{n+2}(\Omega,T)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{n+2}(\Omega,T)}\leq N\|f\|_{\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta+2p}^{n}(\Omega,T)}\,,

(1.9)

where
N=N(d,p,\alpha,n,\theta,\lambda,M_{\lambda})
.

For a comparison between the function spaces in Theorem
1.2
and those in
[
69
]
, see
[
62
, Remark 1.4]
.

2) Convex domains.
A convex domain
\Omega
is an open set such that
(1-t)x+ty\in\Omega
whenever
x,\,y\in\Omega
and
t\in[0,1]
.

Theorem 1.3

(see Corollary
5.15
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a convex (possibly unbounded) domain.
Then for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying

\displaystyle-p-1<\theta-d<-1\,,

if
f\in\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta+2p}^{n}(\Omega,T)
, then equation (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) with any ellipticity constant
0<\nu\leq 1
in (
1.7
) has a unique solution
u
in
\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{n+2}(\Omega,T)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta}^{n+2}(\Omega,T)}\leq N\|f\|_{\mathbb{W}_{p,\theta+2p}^{n}(\Omega,T)}\,,

(1.10)

where
N=N(d,p,\alpha,n,\theta)
.
In particular,
N
is independent of
\Omega
.

3) Totally vanishing exterior Reifenberg condition.

The totally vanishing exterior Reifenberg condition (abbreviated as
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
) extends the concept of bounded vanishing Reifenberg domains introduced in (
5.45
) and the discussion below.

To clarify the main concept of
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
in Definition
5.16
.(3), we present a simplified version in Definition
1.4
, denoted by
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{*}
.
Note that
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{*}
is a sufficient condition for
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
.
In Figure
5.4
, we illustrate the differences between the vanishing Reifenberg condition,
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{*}
, and
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
.

Definition 1.4
.

We say that an open set
\Omega
satisfies
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{*}
if for each
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exist
R_{0,\delta},\,R_{\infty,\delta}>0
satisfying the following: For every
p\in\partial\Omega
and
r>0
with
r\leq R_{0,\delta}
or
r\geq R_{\infty,\delta}
, there exists a unit vector
e_{p,r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\Omega\cap B_{r}(p)\subset\{x\in B_{r}(p)\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p,r}<\delta r\}\,.

As shown in Example
5.18
,
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{*}
is satisfied by bounded domains of the following types: the vanishing Reifenberg domains,
C^{1}
-domains, domains satisfying the exterior ball condition, and finite intersections of these domains.
Furthermore, several unbounded domains also satisfy
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{*}
(see (
5.47
)).

Theorem 1.5

(see Corollary
5.20
)
.

Suppose that
\Omega
satisfies
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{*}
.
Then for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying

\displaystyle-p-1<\theta-d<-1\,,

the same assertion as that in Theorem
1.3
holds.
Here,
N
in (
1.10
) depends only on
d
,
p
,
n
,
\theta
, and
\big\{R_{0,\delta}/R_{\infty,\delta}\big\}_{\delta\in(0,1]}
.

Parabolic equations in bounded vanishing Reifenberg domains have been investigated in the literature, such as Byun and Wang
[
8
]
and Choi and Kim
[
10
]
.
These studies mainly focused on equations with variable coefficients, and also provided weighted
L_{p}
-estimates for Muckenhoupt
A_{p}
-weight functions.
However, these studies mostly dealt with bounded vanishing Reifenberg domains.
In contrast with these works, Theorem
1.5
considers domains satisfying
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{\ast}
, thereby including bounded vanishing Reifenberg domains.

4) Lipschitz cones.

Lipschitz cones are defined by
\Omega:=\{r\sigma\,:\,r>0\,\,,\,\,\,\,\sigma\in\mathcal{M}\}
with
\mathcal{M}
a Lipschitz subdomain of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
In Section
5.5
, we derive a pointwise estimate for homogeneous solutions of the equation

\displaystyle u_{t}=\Delta u\quad\text{in}\quad(0,1]\times\big(B_{1}\cap\Omega\big)\quad;\quad u=0\quad\text{on}\quad(0,1]\times\big(B_{1}\cap\partial\Omega\big)\,,

where
B_{1}:=B_{1}(0)
(see Theorem
5.25
and equation (
5.72
)).

5) Domains with the capacity density condition.

We consider domains
\Omega
satisfying

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\partial\Omega\\
r>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathrm{Cap}\left(\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}_{r}(p),B_{2r}(p)\right)}{\mathrm{Cap}\left(\overline{B}_{r}(p),B_{2r}(p)\right)}>0\,,

(1.11)

where
\mathrm{Cap}(K,U)
denotes the
L_{2}
-capacity of
K
relative to
U
(for the definition, see (
5.6
)).
Condition (
1.11
) has been studied extensively in the literature, including
[
2
,
3
,
28
,
35
,
47
]
.
In particular, the volume density condition (
1.4
) is a sufficient condition for (
1.11
).
As an application, we consider the equation

\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=\Delta u+f_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}\quad\text{in}\quad(0,T]\times\Omega\quad;\quad u(0,x)=0\quad\text{for}\quad x\in\Omega

for a domain
\Omega
satisfying (
1.11
).
We establish an unweighted
L_{p}
solvability result for
p
near
2
(see Theorem
5.10
), and prove Hölder continuity of the solution under the assumption that

|f_{0}|\lesssim\rho^{-2+\epsilon}
and
|f_{1}|+\cdots+|f_{d}|\lesssim\rho^{-1+\epsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
(see Theorem
5.11
).

6) Domains
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(\Omega^{c})<d-2
.

Recall the definition of the Aikawa dimension,
\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(E)
, given in (
5.37
).

Theorem 1.6

(see Corollary
5.13
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 3
, satisfy
\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(\Omega^{c})=:\beta_{0}<d-2
.
Then for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying

-d+\beta_{0}<\theta<(p-1)(d-\beta_{0})-2p\,,

the same assertion as that in Theorem
1.2
holds.
Here,
N
in (
1.9
) depends only on
d
,
p
,
n
,
\theta
,
\beta_{0}
,
\{A_{\beta}\}_{\beta>\beta_{0}}
in (
5.38
).

1.3.
Notations.

•

We use all notations introduced in
[
62
, Subsection 1.3]
.

•

The letter
N
denotes a finite positive constant which may have different values along the argument, whose dependence will be specified in context;
N=N(a,b,\cdots)
means that this
N
depends only on the parameters inside the parentheses.

•

For a list of parameters
L
,
A\lesssim_{L}B
means that
A\leq N(L)B
, and
A\simeq_{L}B
means that
A\lesssim_{L}B
and
B\lesssim_{L}A
.

•

a\vee b:=\max\{a,b\}
,
a\wedge b:=\min\{a,b\}
.

•

For a Lebesgue measurable set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
|E|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
E
.

•

For
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
,
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d})
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
x\cdot y:=(x,y)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}:=\sum^{d}_{i=1}x_{i}y_{i}
denotes the standard inner product.

•

B_{r}(p):=B(p,r):=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,|x-p|<r\big\}
, and
B_{r}:=B_{r}(0)
.

•

For a fixed open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we usually denote
\rho(x):=d(x,\partial\mathcal{O})
when there is no confusion.

•

For a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
1_{E}
denotes the function defined by
1_{E}(x)=1
for
x\in E
, and
1_{E}(x)=0
for
x\notin E
.
For a function
f
defined in
E
,
f1_{E}
denotes the function defined as
\big(f1_{E}\big)(x)=f(x)
if
x\in E
, and
\big(f1_{E}\big)(x)=0
if
x\notin E
.

•

For an open set
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})
denotes the set of all distributions on
\mathcal{O}
, which is the dual of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O})
.
For
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O})
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{O})
, the expression
\langle f,\varphi\rangle
denotes the evaluation of
f
with the test function
\varphi
.

•

For any multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})
,
\alpha_{i}\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
,

\partial_{t}f:=\frac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}t}\,\,,\,\,\,\,\partial_{t}^{n}f:=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}f}{\mathrm{d}t^{n}}\,\,,\,\,\,\,D_{i}f:=\frac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}x_{i}}\,\,,\,\,\,\,D^{\alpha}_{x}f(x):=D^{\alpha_{1}}_{1}\cdots D^{\alpha_{d}}_{d}f(x).

We denote
|\alpha|:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}
.
For the second order derivatives we denote
D_{i}D_{j}f
by
D_{ij}f
. We often abbreviate

|gf_{x}|:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|gD_{i}f|
,
|gf_{xx}|:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}|gD_{ij}f|
, and
\big|gD^{m}_{x}f\big|:=\sum_{|\alpha|=m}|gD^{\alpha}_{x}f|
.
We extend these notations to a sublinear function
\|\cdot\|:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
; for example,
\|gf_{x}\|:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|gD_{i}f\|
.

•

\Delta f:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{ii}f
denotes the Laplacian of a function (or distribution)
f
defined on an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

•

For a measure space
(A,\mathcal{A},\mu)
, a Banach space
(B,\|\cdot\|_{B})
, and
p\in[1,\infty]
, we write
L_{p}(A,\mathcal{A},\mu;B)
for the set of all
B
-valued
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable functions
f
satisfying

\displaystyle\|f\|^{p}_{L_{p}(A,\mathcal{A},\mu;B)}:=\int_{A}\lVert f\rVert^{p}_{B}\,\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty

\displaystyle\qquad\text{if}\quad p\in[1,\infty)\,;

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(A,\mathcal{A},\mu;B)}:=\underset{x\in A}{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}\,\|f(x)\|_{B}<\infty

\displaystyle\qquad\text{if}\quad p=\infty\,.

Here,
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
is the completion of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
\mu
.
We will drop
\mathcal{A}
or
\mu
or even
B
in
L_{p}(A,\mathcal{A},\mu;B)
when they are obvious in the context.

2.
Preliminary

Throughout this section,
\Omega
is an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(with
d\in\mathbb{N}
), and
\rho(x)
denotes the boundary distance function
d(x,\partial\Omega)
.

2.1.
Fractional calculus

We recall the definitions of the Riemann–Liouville fractional integral and the Caputo fractional derivative.

Definition 2.1
.

(1)

Let
\alpha>0
.
For a measurable function
f:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
I_{t}^{\alpha}f
denotes the Riemann–Liouville fractional integral defined by

I_{t}^{\alpha}f(t_{0}):=\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\frac{(t_{0}-s)^{-1+\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

provided that the right-hand side is well-defined,
i.e.
,
(t_{0}-\,\cdot\,)^{-1+\alpha}f(\,\cdot\,)\in L_{1}\big((0,t_{0}]\big)
. We also define
I_{t}^{0}f(t)=f(t)
.

(2)

Let
0<\alpha<1
.
For
f\in C^{1}\big([0,T]\big)
,
\partial^{\alpha}_{t}f
denotes the Caputo fractional derivative defined by
\partial^{\alpha}_{t}f:=\partial_{t}I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\big(f-f(0)\big)=I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\big(\partial_{t}f\big)
.

Using the following relation between the beta function and the gamma function:

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}(t-r)^{-1+\beta_{1}}(r-s)^{-1+\beta_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{\Gamma(\beta_{1})\Gamma(\beta_{2})}{\Gamma(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}(t-s)^{-1+\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}\,,

(2.1)

for all
\beta_{1},\,\beta_{2}>0
, we obtain that for any
\beta_{1},\,\beta_{2}\geq 0
,
I_{t}^{\beta_{1}}\big(I_{t}^{\beta_{2}}f\big)=I_{t}^{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}f
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
F\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}\big([0,T)\big)
,
p\in(1,\infty)
, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Then

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}\big(|F|^{p}\big)(t)\leq p|F(t)|^{p-2}F(t)\partial_{t}^{\alpha}F(t)\,.

(2.2)

Moreover, if
F(0)=0
, then

\displaystyle 0\leq I^{1-\alpha}_{t}\big(|F|^{p}\big)(t)\leq p\int_{0}^{t}|F(s)|^{p-2}F(s)\partial_{t}^{\alpha}F(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,.

(2.3)

When
p=2
, this lemma is implicit in the proof of
[
13
, Proposition 4.1]
.
We extend the argument to general
1<p<\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

By the definition of
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}:=I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle J(t):=\,

\displaystyle p|F(t)|^{p-2}F(t)\partial_{t}^{\alpha}F(t)-\partial_{t}^{\alpha}\big(|F|^{p}\big)(t)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle p\int_{0}^{t}\frac{(t-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\Big(|F(t)|^{p-2}F(t)F^{\prime}(s)-|F(s)|^{p-2}F(s)F^{\prime}(s)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}s.

For a fixed
t\in(0,T]
, put

I(s):=|F(s)|^{p}-|F(t)|^{p}-p|F(t)|^{p-2}F(t)\big(F(s)-F(t)\big)\,.

Observe that

\displaystyle 0\leq I(s)\leq(t-s)\cdot 2p\cdot\|F\|_{\infty}^{p-1}\|F^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\qquad\text{and}\qquad

(2.4)

\displaystyle I^{\prime}(s)=p\Big(|F(s)|^{p-2}F(s)F^{\prime}(s)-|F(t)|^{p-2}F(t)F^{\prime}(s)\Big)\,,

where the first inequality in (
2.4
) follows from the convexity of the map
t\mapsto|t|^{p}
.
These imply that

\displaystyle J(t)=\,

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{(t-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}I^{\prime}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\bigg(\frac{\epsilon^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}I(t-\epsilon)-\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}I(0)-\alpha\int_{0}^{t-\epsilon}\frac{(t-s)^{-1-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}I(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}I(0)+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{(t-s)^{-1-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}I(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,.

Since
I(s)\geq 0
, we have
J(t)\geq 0
, and therefore (
2.2
) follows.

Since

I_{t}^{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}\big(|F|^{p}\big)=I_{t}^{1-\alpha}I_{t}^{1}\partial_{t}\big(|F|^{p}\big)=I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\big(|F(\cdot)|^{p}-|F(0)|^{p}\big)\,,

(
2.3
) follows from (
2.2
).
∎

2.2.
Superharmonic function

The weight system in the main theorem (Theorem
4.8
) consists of the boundary distance function and a superharmonic function.
We recall the definitions of a superharmonic function and a classical superharmonic function.

A locally integrable function
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called a
superharmonic
if
\Delta\phi\leq 0
in the sense of distributions,
i.e.
, for any nonnegative
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\int_{\Omega}\phi\,\Delta\zeta\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 0\,.

A function
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow(-\infty,+\infty]
is called a
classical superharmonic function
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

\phi
is lower semi-continuous in
\Omega
.

(2)

For any
x\in\Omega
and any
r>0
such that
\overline{B}_{r}(x)\subset\Omega
,

\displaystyle\phi(x)\geq\frac{1}{\big|B_{r}(x)\big|}\int_{B_{r}(x)}\phi(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

(3)

\phi\not\equiv+\infty
on each connected component of
\Omega
.

As shown in
[
4
]
,
\phi
is harmonic if and only if both
\phi
and
-\phi
are classical superharmonic functions.

We collect several lemmas for superharmonic functions and classical superharmonic functions.
The proofs of Lemmas
2.3
-
2.5
are given in
[
62
, Section 2]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

A function
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow[-\infty,+\infty]
is superharmonic if and only if there exists a classical superharmonic function
\phi_{0}
in
\Omega
such that
\phi=\phi_{0}
almost everywhere in
\Omega
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\phi
be a classical superharmonic function in
\Omega
.

(1)

If
\phi
is twice continuously differentiable, then
\Delta\phi\leq 0
.

(2)

\phi
is locally integrable in
\Omega
.

(3)

For any compact set
K\subset\Omega
,
\phi
has the minimum value on
K
.

(4)

For
\epsilon>0
, put

\displaystyle\phi^{(\epsilon)}(x)=\int_{B_{1}(0)}\big(\phi 1_{\Omega}\big)(x-\epsilon y)\cdot N_{0}\,\mathrm{e}^{-1/(1-|y|^{2})}\,\mathrm{d}y\,,

(2.5)

where
N_{0}:=\big(\int_{B_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-1/(1-|y|^{2})}\,\mathrm{d}y\big)^{-1}
.
Then for any compact set
K\subset\Omega
and
0<\epsilon<d(K,\Omega^{c})
, the following statements hold:

(a)

\phi^{(\epsilon)}
is infinitely differentiable on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(b)

\phi^{(\epsilon)}
is a classical superharmonic function in
K^{\circ}
.

(c)

For any
x\in K
,
\phi^{(\epsilon)}(x)\nearrow\phi(x)
as
\epsilon\searrow 0
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\phi
be a positive superharmonic function in
\Omega
, and denote by
\phi^{(\epsilon)}
the function defined in (
2.5
).

(1)

For any
c\leq 1
,
\phi^{c}
is locally integrable in
\Omega
.

(2)

If
f\in L_{1}(\Omega)
and
\mathrm{supp}(f)
is a compact subset of
\Omega
, then for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}|f|\big(\phi^{(\epsilon)}\big)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}|f|\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(3)

If
f\in L_{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\mathrm{supp}(f)
is a compact subset of
\Omega
, then for any
c\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}f\big(\phi^{(\epsilon)}\big)^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

2.3.
Harnack function and regular Harnack function

One of our primary methods is the localization argument used in
[
40
]
.
We employ the notions of the Harnack function and the regular Harnack function to extend this argument to various weight systems.

Definition 2.6
.

(1)

A measurable function
\psi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is called a
Harnack function
if there exists a constant
C=:\mathrm{C}_{1}(\psi)>0
such that

\displaystyle\underset{B(x,\rho(x)/2)}{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}\,\psi\leq C\underset{B(x,\rho(x)/2)}{\mathrm{ess\,inf}}\,\psi\quad\text{for all}\,\,x\in\Omega\,.

(2.6)

(2)

A function
\Psi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
is called a
regular Harnack function
if
\Psi>0
and there exists a sequence of constants
\big(C^{(k)}\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}=:\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)
such that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle|D_{x}^{k}\Psi|\leq C^{(k)}\,\rho^{-k}\Psi\quad\text{on}\quad\Omega\,.

(3)

Let
\psi
be a measurable function and
\Psi
be a regular Harnack function in
\Omega
.

\Psi
is called a
regularization
of
\psi
if there exists a constant
C=:\mathrm{C}_{3}(\psi,\Psi)>0
such that

C^{-1}\Psi\leq\psi\leq C\,\Psi\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\,\,\Omega.

It is worth mentioning that, as shown in
[
62
, Lemma 3.4]
, (
2.6
) is equivalent to the existence of constants
r\in(0,1)
and
N_{r}>0
such that

\displaystyle\underset{B(x,r\rho(x))}{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}\,\psi\leq N_{r}\underset{B(x,r\rho(x))}{\mathrm{ess\,inf}}\,\psi\quad\text{for all}\,\,x\in\Omega\,.

(2.7)

The following example and lemma are given in
[
62
, Subsection 3.1]
:

Example 2.7
.

(1)

For any
E\subset\Omega^{c}
, the function
x\mapsto d(x,E)
is a Harnack function in
\Omega
.
In addition,
\mathrm{C}_{1}\big(d(\,\cdot\,,E)\big)
can be chosen as
3
.

(2)

If
\Psi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
satisfies
\Psi>0
and
\Delta\Psi=-\Lambda\Psi
for some constant
\Lambda\geq 0
, then
\Psi
is a regular Harnack function in
\Omega
.
In addition,
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)
can be chosen to depend only on
d
.

(3)

For any regular Harnack function
\Psi
in
\Omega
, and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Psi^{\sigma}
is also a regular Harnack function in
\Omega
.
In addition,
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi^{\sigma})
can be chosen to depend only on
d
,
\sigma
,
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)
.

(4)

Let
\Psi
and
\Phi
be regularizations of
\psi
and
\phi
, respectively.
Then
\Psi\Phi
,
\Psi+\Phi
, and
\frac{\Phi\Psi}{\Phi+\Psi}
are regularizations of
\psi\phi
,
\max(\psi,\phi)
, and
\min(\psi,\phi)
, respectively.

Lemma 2.8
.

(1)

If
\psi
is a Harnack function,
\psi
admits a regularization.
For this regularization of
\psi
denoted by
\widetilde{\psi}
,
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\widetilde{\psi})
and
\mathrm{C}_{3}(\psi,\widetilde{\psi})
can be chosen to depend only on
d
and
\mathrm{C}_{1}(\psi)
.

(2)

If
\Psi
is a regular Harnack function, then it is also a Harnack function and
\mathrm{C}_{1}(\Psi)
can be chosen to depend only on
d
and
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)
.

We conclude this subsection with a lemma describing the boundary behavior of regular Harnack functions in a John domain.
Recall that a domain
\Omega
is called a John domain it satisfies the following condition:

There exist a point
x_{0}\in\Omega
and constants
L_{0},\,\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
x\in\Omega
, there exists a rectifiable curve
\gamma:[0,L]\rightarrow\Omega
, parametrized by arclength, such that

L\leq L_{0}
,
\gamma(0)=x
,
\gamma(L)=x_{0}
, and
d(\gamma(t),\partial\Omega)\geq\epsilon_{0}t/L
for all
t\in[0,L]
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\Omega
be a John domain.
For each fixed
x_{0}
, there exist constants
N,\,A>0
such that for every regular Harnack function
\Psi
in
\Omega
,

N^{-1}\rho(x)^{A}\leq\frac{\Psi(x)}{\Psi(x_{0})}\leq N\rho(x)^{-A}\quad\text{for every}\quad x\in\Omega\,.

Proof.

Let
\Psi
be a regular Harnack function in
\Omega
.
Then
\Psi
is also a continuous Harnack function (see Lemma
2.8
.(2)).
It follows from
[
67
, Corollary 3.4]
that for an arbitrary domain
\Omega
,

\displaystyle N_{0}^{-(k(x,x_{0})+1)}\leq\frac{\Psi(x)}{\Psi(x_{0})}\leq N_{0}^{k(x,x_{0})+1}\qquad\text{for all}\quad x_{0},\,x\in\Omega\,,

(2.8)

where
N_{0}\geq 1
is a constant depending only on
\mathrm{C}_{1}(\Psi)
.
Here,
k(x,x_{0})\geq 0
denotes the quasihyperbolic distance between
x
and
x_{0}
(see
[
67
, paragraph 2.5]
for the definition).
In addition, Gehring and Martio
[
18
, Theorem 3.11]
proved that if
\Omega
is a John domain, then for every
x_{0}\in\Omega
there exists
A,\,B>0
depending only on
\Omega
and
x_{0}
such that

\displaystyle k(x,x_{0})\leq A\log\frac{1}{\rho(x)}+B\qquad\text{whenever}\quad x\in\Omega\,.

(2.9)

Combining (
2.8
) and (
2.9
), we complete the proof.
∎

2.4.
Weighted function spaces

In this subsection, we introduce the Krylov-type weighted Sobolev space
H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
and its generalization
\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
.

The theory of
H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
was initiated by Krylov
[
39
,
40
,
42
]
and further developed by Lototsky
[
48
,
49
]
.
These developments were motivated by stochastic parabolic equations and aimed at controlling the boundary behavior of derivatives of solutions, as discussed in detail in
[
38
,
40
]
.
In
[
62
]
, the present author proposed a generalization, denoted by
\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, by introducing Harnack functions as weights.

We suppose that
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\gamma,\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Omega
is an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\Psi
is a regular Harnack function in
\Omega
.
For brevity, we write

\mathbf{I}=\{d,\,p,\,\gamma,\,\theta,\,\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)\}\,,

where
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)
denotes the constant appearing in Definition
2.6
.

We first recall the definition of Bessel potential spaces and Besov spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the space of Bessel potentials with the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\|(1-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big[(1+|\xi|^{2})^{\gamma/2}\mathcal{F}(f)(\xi)\big]\big\|_{p}\,,

where
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
denote the Fourier transform and its inverse, respectively.
For
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, by
B_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we denote the Besov space whose norm is given by

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\big\|(1-\Delta)^{(\gamma-1)/2}f\big\|_{B_{p}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,,

where

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B_{p}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}:=\|f\|_{p}^{p}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x+h)-2f(x)+f(x-h)|^{p}}{|h|^{d+p}}\,\mathrm{d}h\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

It is known that
H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
coincides with the Triebel-Lizorkin space
F_{p,2}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
B_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
coincides with
B_{p,p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=F_{p,p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see
[
65
, Subsection 2.3.5, Theorem 2.3.8]
).
For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
s\in(0,1)
, the Bessel potential space
H_{p}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is equivalent to the Sobolev space

\displaystyle W_{p}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\bigg\{f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\,\sum_{k=0}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{x}^{k}f|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\bigg\}\,,

and
B_{p}^{n+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
coincides with the Sobolev-Slobodeckij space,

\displaystyle W_{p}^{n+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\,

\displaystyle\Big\{f\in W_{p}^{n}\,:\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\{y:|y-x|<1\}}\frac{|D_{x}^{n}f(x)-D_{x}^{n}f(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\Big\}\,.

Throughout this subsection,
X=H
or
B
, so that
\big(X_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega),\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)\big)
stands for either

\big(H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega),\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)\big)\quad\text{or}\quad\big(B_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega),\Psi B_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)\big)\,.

To define the spaces
X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
and
\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
,
take
\widetilde{\rho}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exists
N_{k}=N(d,k)>0
such that

\displaystyle N_{0}^{-1}\rho(x)\leq\widetilde{\rho}(x)\leq N_{0}\rho(x)\quad\text{and}\quad|D_{x}^{k}\widetilde{\rho}(x)|\leq N_{k}\,\widetilde{\rho}(x)^{-k+1}\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\Omega\,.

(see,
e.g.
, Lemma
2.8
.(1)).
Take
\zeta_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that

\displaystyle\zeta_{0}\geq 0\,\,,\,\,\,\ \text{supp}(\zeta_{0})\subset[\mathrm{e}^{-1},\mathrm{e}]\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\zeta_{0}(\mathrm{e}^{n}\,\cdot\,)\equiv 1\quad\text{on}\,\,\mathbb{R}_{+}\,.

For arbitrary
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we denote

\displaystyle\xi_{(n)}(x):=\xi(\mathrm{e}^{-n}\widetilde{\rho}(x))\,.

Note that the following properties hold for
\zeta_{0,(n)}:=\zeta_{0}\big(\mathrm{e}^{-n}\widetilde{\rho}(\,\cdot\,)\big)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\zeta_{0,(n)}\equiv 1\quad\text{on}\,\,\Omega\,,\\
&\text{supp}(\zeta_{0,(n)})\subset\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\mathrm{e}^{n-1}\leq\widetilde{\rho}(x)\leq\mathrm{e}^{n+1}\}\,,\\
&\zeta_{0,(n)}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{and}\quad|D_{x}^{\alpha}\zeta_{0,(n)}|\leq N(d,\alpha,\zeta)\,\mathrm{e}^{-n|\alpha|}\,.\end{split}

Definition 2.10
.

(1)

By
X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
we denote the class of all distributions
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle\|f\|^{p}_{X^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta}\|\big(\zeta_{0,(n)}f\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot)\|_{X^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}<\infty\,.

(2)

By
\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
we denote the class of all distributions
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
such that
f=\Psi g
for some
g\in X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
. The norm in
\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
is defined by

\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}:=\|\Psi^{-1}f\|_{X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,.

We also denote
L_{p,\theta}(\Omega):=H_{p,\theta}^{0}(\Omega)
and
\Psi L_{p,\theta}(\Omega):=\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{0}(\Omega)
.

The following equivalent norm for
\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
was established in
[
62
, Lemma 3.12]
.

Lemma 2.11
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{n}(\Omega)}^{p}\simeq_{\mathbf{I}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\int_{\Omega}|\rho^{k}D_{x}^{k}f|^{p}\Psi^{-p}\rho^{\theta-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(2.10)

Proposition
A.1
shows that, for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
s\in(0,1)
,

\|f\|_{\Psi B_{p,\theta}^{n+s}(\Omega)}\simeq\|f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{n}(\Omega)}+[D_{x}^{n}f]_{\Psi B_{p,\theta+np}^{s}(\Omega)}\,,

where

\displaystyle[g]_{\Psi B_{p,\widetilde{\theta}}^{s}(\Omega)}^{p}:=\int_{\Omega}\bigg(\int_{h:|h|<\frac{\rho(x)}{2}}\frac{|g(x+h)-g(x)|^{p}}{|h|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}h\bigg)\Psi(x)^{p}\rho(x)^{sp+\widetilde{\theta}-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(2.11)

We now summarize the properties of
\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
.
Since
\zeta_{0}
is fixed and the spaces
X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
are independent of the choice of
\zeta_{0}
(see (
2.22
)), we omit the dependence on
\zeta_{0}
.

Most properties of
\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
in Lemmas
2.12
–
2.16
are proved in
[
62
]
, while the corresponding results for
\Psi B_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
can be derived in a similar manner.
However, Lemmas
2.12
.(4)-(5) and
2.13
.(1)-(3), (9) are not covered in
[
62
]
, and we focus on these cases below.
It is worth noting that the complete proofs of Lemmas
2.12
-
2.16
are provided also in
[
61
, Subsection 3.2, Appendix A]
.

Lemma 2.12
.

(1)

\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
is a Banach space.

(2)

C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
.

(3)

\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
is a reflexive Banach space with the dual
\Psi X_{p^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}^{-\gamma}(\Omega)
, where

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\theta}{p}+\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}}=d\,.

(2.12)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega),\,g\neq 0}\frac{\big|\langle f,g\rangle\big|}{\|g\|_{\Psi X_{p^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}^{-\gamma}(\Omega)}}\simeq_{\mathbf{I}}\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,.

(2.13)

(4)

Let
p_{i}\in(1,\infty)
and
\gamma_{i}
,
\theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i=0,\,1
.
For each
t\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\big[\Psi X^{\gamma_{0}}_{p_{0},\theta_{0}}(\Omega),\Psi X^{\gamma_{1}}_{p_{1},\theta_{1}}(\Omega)\big]_{t}\simeq_{N}\Psi X^{\gamma_{t}}_{p_{t},\theta_{t}}(\Omega)\,,

(2.14)

where
N=N(d,p_{i},\theta_{i},\gamma_{i},t;i=1,2)
.
Here,
[Y_{0},Y_{1}]_{t}
is the complex interpolation space of
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
(see
[
65
, Section 1.9]
for the definition and properties of the complex interpolation spaces), and the parameters
p_{t}\in(1,\infty)
and
\gamma_{t},\,\theta_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p_{t}}=\frac{1-t}{p_{0}}+\frac{t}{p_{1}}\,\,,\,\,\,\,\gamma_{t}=(1-t)\gamma_{0}+t\gamma_{1}\,\,\,,\,\,\,\,\frac{\theta_{t}}{p_{t}}=(1-t)\frac{\theta_{0}}{p_{0}}+t\frac{\theta_{1}}{p_{1}}\,.

(2.15)

(5)

Let
p_{i}\in(1,\infty)
and
\gamma_{i}
,
\theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i=0,\,1
, with
\gamma_{0}\neq\gamma_{1}
.
For each
t\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\big(\Psi X^{\gamma_{0}}_{p_{0},\theta_{0}}(\Omega),\Psi X^{\gamma_{1}}_{p_{1},\theta_{1}}(\Omega)\big)_{t,p_{t}}\simeq_{N}\Psi B^{\gamma_{t}}_{p_{t},\theta_{t}}(\Omega)

(2.16)

where
N=N(d,p_{i},\theta_{i},\gamma_{i},t;i=1,2)
.
Here,
(Y_{0},Y_{1})_{t,p_{t}}
is the real interpolation space of
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
(see
[
65
, Section 1.3]
for the definition and properties of the real interpolation spaces), and the parameters
p_{t}\in(1,\infty)
and
\gamma_{t},\,\theta_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy (
2.15
).

Proof.

(4) and (5) follow from the interpolation properties of
X_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see
[
66
, Section 2.4]
) and the result in
[
65
, Theorem 1.2.4]
, where the latter is applied to the maps

S^{\Psi}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}:=\Big\{(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\Big\}\,\,,\,\,S^{\Psi}f:=\Big(\big(\Psi^{-1}f\zeta_{0,(n)}\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\,\cdot\,)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,;

R^{\Psi}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\quad,\quad R^{\Psi}\{f_{n}\}:=\Psi\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\zeta_{1,(n)}(\,\cdot\,)f_{n}(\mathrm{e}^{-n}\,\cdot\,)\,,

where
\zeta_{1}(\,\cdot\,)=\zeta_{0}(\mathrm{e}^{-1}\,\cdot\,)+\zeta_{0}(\,\cdot\,)+\zeta_{0}(\mathrm{e}\,\cdot\,)
.
Note that for any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\theta,\,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
S^{\Psi}
is bounded from
\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
to
\ell^{p}_{\theta}\big(H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, while
R^{\Psi}
is bounded from
\ell^{p}_{\theta}\big(H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
to
\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, where

\ell^{p}_{\theta}\big(H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big):=\Big\{(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,\big\|(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\big\|_{\ell^{p}_{\theta}(\mathbb{Z};H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{p}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta}\|f_{n}\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\Big\}\,.

Moreover,
R^{\Psi}\circ S^{\Psi}
is the identity map on
\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
.
For more detailed proof, see
[
61
, Proposition A.2]
.
∎

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
0<\alpha\leq 1
, and
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\displaystyle|f|_{k}^{(\delta)}:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\big\|\rho^{\delta+i}D_{x}^{i}f\big\|_{C(\Omega)}\quad,\quad|f|_{k,\alpha}^{(\delta)}\,\,\,\,

\displaystyle:=|f|_{k}^{(\delta)}+[\,\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta+k+\alpha}D_{x}^{k}f\,]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Omega)}\,.

It is worth noting that for any regular Harnack function
\Psi
,

\displaystyle|\Psi^{-1}f|_{k}^{(\delta)}

\displaystyle\simeq_{N_{1}}\sum_{i=0}^{k}\sup_{\Omega}\left|\Psi^{-1}\rho^{\delta+i}D_{x}^{i}f\right|\,,

(2.17)

\displaystyle|\Psi^{-1}f|_{k,\alpha}^{(\delta)}

\displaystyle\simeq_{N_{2}}|\Psi^{-1}f|_{k}^{(\delta)}+\sup_{x\in\Omega}\bigg(\Psi^{-1}(x)\rho^{\delta+k+\alpha}(x)\sup_{y:|y-x|<\frac{\rho(x)}{2}}\frac{\left|D_{x}^{k}f(x)-D_{x}^{k}f(y)\right|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}\bigg)\,,

(2.18)

where
N_{1}=N\big(d,k,\delta,\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)\big)
and
N_{2}=N\big(d,k,\alpha,\delta,\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)\big)
.
(
2.17
) follows by direct calculation, and (
2.18
) is proved in
[
62
, Proposition 3.13]
.

Lemma 2.13
.

(1)

If
1<p\leq 2
(resp.
p\geq 2
), then

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\mathbf{I}}\,(\text{resp.}\gtrsim_{\mathbf{I}})\,\|f\|_{\Psi B_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,.

(2.19)

(2)

For any
s<\gamma
,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{s}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{\Psi B_{p,\theta}^{s}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\mathbf{I},s}\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,.

(2.20)

(3)

Let
p_{i}\in(1,\infty)
and
\gamma_{i},\,\theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i=0,\,1
, such that

\gamma_{0}>\gamma_{1}\,\,,\quad\gamma_{0}-\frac{d}{p_{0}}=\gamma_{1}-\frac{d}{p_{1}}\,\,,\quad\frac{\theta_{0}}{p_{0}}=\frac{\theta_{1}}{p_{1}}\,.

Then

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p_{1},\theta_{1}}^{\gamma_{1}}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{\Psi B_{p_{1},\theta_{1}}^{\gamma_{1}}(\Omega)}\leq N\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p_{0},\theta_{0}}^{\gamma_{0}}(\Omega)}

(2.21)

where
N=N(d,p_{i},\gamma_{i},\theta_{i};i=0,1)
.

(4)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k>|\gamma|
, if
a\in C^{k,(0)}(\Omega)
, then

\displaystyle\|af\|_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\mathbf{I}}\|a\|_{C^{k,(0)}(\Omega)}\|f\|_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}\,.

(5)

For any
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta}\big\|\big(\eta_{(n)}f\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot)\big\|^{p}_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p}}\lesssim_{\mathbf{I},\eta}\|f\|_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}^{p}\,.

If
\eta
additionally satisfies

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\Big[\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta(\mathrm{e}^{n}t)\Big]>0\,,

then

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}^{p}\simeq_{\mathbf{I},\eta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta}\big\|\big(\eta_{(n)}f\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot)\big\|^{p}_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

(2.22)

(6)

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\rho}^{\,s}f\|_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}\simeq_{\mathbf{I},s}\|f\|_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p,\theta+sp}(\Omega)}\,.

(2.23)

(7)

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\simeq_{\mathbf{I},k}\sum_{i=0}^{k}\|D_{x}^{i}f\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta+ip}^{\gamma-k}(\Omega)}\,.

(2.24)

(8)

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
0<\alpha<1
satisfy
\gamma-d/p>k+\alpha
.
Then for any
f\in\Psi H^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\left|\Psi^{-1}f\right|^{(\theta/p)}_{k,\alpha}\leq_{\mathbf{I},k,\alpha}\|f\|_{\Psi X^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}\,.

(2.25)

(9)

Let
t\in(0,1)
, and let
p_{i}\in(1,\infty),\,\theta_{i},\,\gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
(
i=0,\,1,\,,\,t
) be constants satisfying (
2.15
).
Then

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p_{t},\theta_{t}}^{\gamma_{t}}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{N}\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p_{0},\theta_{0}}^{\gamma_{0}}(\Omega)}^{1-t}\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p_{1},\theta_{1}}^{\gamma_{1}}(\Omega)}^{t}\,,

(2.26)

where
N=N(\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi),\gamma_{i},p_{i},\theta_{i},t;i=1,2)
.

(10)

For any
k,\,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and a regular Harnack function
\Phi
in
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\big\|\big(D_{x}^{k}\Phi\big)D_{x}^{l}f\big\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|\Phi f\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta-(k+l)p}^{\gamma+l}(\Omega)}\,,

(2.27)

where
N=N(\mathbf{I},\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Phi),k,l)
.

Proof.

(1) This follows from the fact that
B_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if
1<p\leq 2
and
H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset B_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if
p\geq 2
(see
[
66
, Proposition 2.3.2/2.(iii)]
).

(2) This follows from the fact that
X_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset H_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap B_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if
\gamma>s
(see
[
66
, Proposition 2.3.2/2.(ii)]
).

(3) Note that
p_{0}<p_{1}
.
Since
X_{p_{0}}^{\gamma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset X_{p_{1}}^{\gamma_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap B_{p_{1}}^{\gamma_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see
[
66
, Theorem 2.7.1]
), we have

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta_{1}}\|\big(f\zeta_{0,(n)}\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot)\|_{X_{p_{1}}^{\gamma_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p_{1}}\Big)^{1/p_{1}}+\Big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta_{1}}\|\big(f\zeta_{0,(n)}\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot)\|_{B_{p_{1}}^{\gamma_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p_{1}}\Big)^{1/p_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle N\Big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta_{0}}\|\big(f\zeta_{0,(n)}\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot)\|_{X_{p_{0}}^{\gamma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p_{1}}\Big)^{1/p_{1}}\leq N\Big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta_{0}}\|\big(f\zeta_{0,(n)}\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\cdot)\|_{X_{p_{0}}^{\gamma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p_{0}}\Big)^{1/p_{0}}\,,

where
N=N(d,p_{i},\gamma_{i};i=0,1)
.

(9) This follows from (
2.14
) in combination with
[
65
, Theorem 1.9.3.(f)]
.
∎

Lemma 2.14
.

There exist bounded linear maps

\Lambda_{0}\,:\,\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}\rightarrow\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+1}(\Omega)\quad\text{and}\quad\,\Lambda_{1},\,\ldots,\,\Lambda_{d}:\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}\rightarrow\Psi H_{p,\theta-p}^{\gamma+1}(\Omega)

such that, for any
f\in\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, we have

f=\Lambda_{0}f+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}(\Lambda_{i}f)

and

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\|\Lambda_{0}f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+1}(\Omega)}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|\Lambda_{i}f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta-p}^{\gamma+1}(\Omega)}\leq N\|f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,,\end{gathered}

where
N=N(d,p,\gamma,\theta,\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi))
.

The following corollary follows directly from (
2.13
) and Lemma
2.14
(see also the proof of
[
62
, Lemma 3.12(2)]
).

Corollary 2.15
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist bounded linear maps
\Lambda_{\alpha}:\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)\rightarrow\Psi X_{p,\theta-|\alpha|p}^{\gamma+k}(\Omega)
, for all multi-indices
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq k
, such that

f=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}D^{\alpha}_{x}\Lambda_{\alpha}f\,.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\simeq_{\mathbf{I},k}\inf\bigg\{\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|f_{\alpha}\|_{\Psi X_{p,\theta-|\alpha|p}^{\gamma+k}(\Omega)}:\,f=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}D_{x}^{\alpha}f_{\alpha}\bigg\}\,.

(2.28)

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy
\eta=1
on
B_{1}(0)
and
\mathrm{supp}(\eta)\subset B_{2}(0)
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
N(i)\in\mathbb{N}
be a constant satisfying

\mathrm{supp}\Big(\sum_{|n|\leq i}\zeta_{0,(n)}\Big)\subset\big\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\big(N(i)/2\big)^{-1}\leq\rho(x)\leq N(i)/2\big\}\,.

Let
\Lambda_{i}
,
\Lambda_{i,j}
,
\Lambda_{i,j,k}
be linear functionals on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
defined as

\displaystyle\Lambda_{i}f:=\Big(\sum_{|n|\leq i}\zeta_{0,(n)}\Big)f\,\,\,,\,\,\,\,\Lambda_{i,j}f=\eta(j^{-1}\cdot\,)\Lambda_{i}f\,\,\,,\,\,\,\,\Lambda_{i,j,k}f=\big(\Lambda_{i,j}f\big)^{(N(i)^{-1}k^{-1})}\,,

where
\big(\Lambda_{i,j}f\big)^{(\epsilon)}
is defined in the same way as in (
2.5
).
Then for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\gamma
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, and regular Harnack function
\Psi
, the following hold:

(1)

For any
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
,
\Lambda_{i,j,k}f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

(2)

For any
f\in\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{i}\|\Lambda_{i}f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\leq N_{1}\|f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\,\sup_{j}\|\Lambda_{i,j}f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\leq N_{2}\|f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}

(2.29)

\displaystyle\,\sup_{k}\|\Lambda_{i,j,k}f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\leq N_{3}\|f\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,,

where
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
,
N_{3}
are constants independent of
f
.

(3)

For any
f\in\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda_{i,j,k}f=\Lambda_{i,j}f\,\,\,,\,\,\,\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda_{i,j}f=\Lambda_{i}f\,\,\,,\,\,\,\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda_{i}f=f\quad\text{in}\quad\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)\,.

(2.30)

3.
Key estimates for the heat equation

For
0<\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}<\infty
and
T\in(0,\infty]
, we denote

•

\mathrm{M}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
: the set of all real symmetric
d\times d
matrices
(\alpha^{ij})_{d\times d}
satisfying

\nu_{1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\alpha^{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\nu_{2}|\xi|^{2}\qquad\forall\,\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,.

•

\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
: the set of all
\mathcal{L}:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a^{ij}(\cdot)D_{ij}
, where
\{a^{ij}(\cdot)\}_{i,j=1,\ldots,d}
is a collection of time-measurable functions in
(0,T)
such that
\big(a^{ij}(t)\big)_{d\times d}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
for all
t\in(0,T)
.

Throughout this section we assume that
\Omega
is an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle T\in(0,\infty)\,\,,\,\,\,\,0<\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}<\infty\,\,,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\,.

(3.1)

We deal with the classical (
\alpha=1
) and time-fractional (
0<\alpha<1
) parabolic equations,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=\mathcal{L}u+f:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a^{ij}(t)D_{ij}u+f\,\,,\quad t\in(0,T]\quad;\quad u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}\,.

(3.2)

Here,
\partial_{t}^{1}
denotes the classical time derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}
, while for
0<\alpha<1
,
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}
denotes the Caputo fractional derivative introduced in Definition
2.1
.

In this paper, our main focus is on the classical and time-fractional heat equations, (
1.1
) and (
1.2
).
For these equations, Lemma 2.6 of
[
62
]
can be applied directly.
However, we also consider the operator
\mathcal{L}
in (
3.2
), which involves variable coefficients.
To treat the operator
\mathcal{L}
, we introduce the following definition:

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\phi
be a positive superharmonic function in
\Omega
.
For
\delta\in(0,1]
and
p\in(1,\infty)
, we denote by
\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,\delta)
the set of all constants
\mu\in(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p})
such that there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}_{4}>0
satisfying the inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\mathrm{C}_{4}\int_{\Omega}\Big(-\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\alpha^{ij}D_{ij}u\Big)\cdot u|u|^{p-2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x

(3.3)

for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
(\alpha^{ij})_{d\times d}\in\mathrm{M}(\delta,1)
.

We use the set
\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,\delta)
to formulate the main theorems, specifically Theorems
3.5
and
4.8
.
According to Lemma
3.3
, if
\delta=1
, corresponding to the case where only the Laplacian
\Delta
is considered, then
\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,1)
is the same as
(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p})
.
Notably, the following proposition guarantees the existence of a non-empty interval contained in
\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,\delta)
, even when
\delta\in(0,1)
, without imposing additional assumptions on
\Omega
,
\phi
, or
p
:

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\delta\in(0,1]
.

(1)

If

\displaystyle\mu\in\Big(-\frac{(p-1)/p}{p(\delta^{-1/2}+1)/2-1},\frac{(p-1)/p}{p(\delta^{-1/2}-1)/2+1}\Big)\,,

(3.4)

then for any positive superharmonic function
\phi
in
\Omega
, it holds that
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,\delta)
and the constant
\mathrm{C}_{4}
in (
3.3
) can be chosen to depend only on
\delta
,
p
, and
\mu
.
In particular,

\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,1)=\left(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\,.

(2)

Suppose
\phi>0
satisfies that for any
(\alpha^{ij})_{d\times d}\in\mathrm{M}(\delta,1)
,
\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\alpha^{ij}D_{ij}\phi\leq 0
in the sense of distributions.
Then

\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,\delta)=\left(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\,.

Moreover, for any
\mu\in(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p})
, the constant
\mathrm{C}_{4}
in (
3.3
) can be chosen to depend only on
\delta
,
p
, and
\mu
.

Proposition
3.2
.(2) is applied to results concerning convex domains and the totally vanishing exterior Reifenberg condition; see Subsections
5.3
and
5.4
, respectively.

The proof of Proposition
3.2
relies on the following two lemmas:

Lemma 3.3

(Lemma 2.6 in
[
62
]
)
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
c<1
, and suppose that
u\in C(\Omega)
satisfies

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{supp}(u)\,\,\text{is a compact subset of}\,\,\,\Omega\,,\\
u\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}\big(\{x\in\Omega\,:\,u(x)\neq 0\}\big)\,\,\,,\,\,\,\text{and}\quad\int_{\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-1}|D_{x}^{2}u|\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\,,\ \end{gathered}

(3.5)

and
\phi
is a positive superharmonic function in a neighborhood of
\mathrm{supp}(u)
.

(1)

If
\phi
is twice continuously differentiable, then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\phi^{c-2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\Big(\frac{p}{1-c}\Big)^{2}\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(3.6)

(2)

If we additionally assume that
c\in(-p+1,1)
and
(\Delta u)1_{\{u\neq 0\}}
is bounded, then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq N\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}(-\Delta u)\cdot u|u|^{p-2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\,,

where
N=N(p,c)>0
.

(3)

If the Hardy inequality (
1.3
) holds for
\Omega
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\phi^{c}\rho^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq N\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\,,

(3.7)

where
N=N(p,c,\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega))>0
.

Lemma 3.4

(Lemma A.1 in
[
62
]
)
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
u\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy (
3.5
).

(1)

|u|^{p/2-1}u\in W_{2}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
D_{i}(|u|^{p/2-1}u)=\frac{p}{2}|u|^{p/2-1}(D_{i}u)1_{\{u\neq 0\}}
.

(2)

|u|^{p}\in W_{1}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{i}\big(|u|^{p}\big)\,&=p|u|^{p-2}uD_{i}u1_{\{u\neq 0\}}\,\,;\\
D_{ij}\big(|u|^{p}\big)\,&=\big(p|u|^{p-2}uD_{ij}u+p(p-1)|u|^{p-2}D_{i}uD_{j}u\big)\,1_{\{u\neq 0\}}\,.\end{split}

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

(1) Let
\mu
satisfy (
3.4
).
Our goal is to prove (
3.3
) for each
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with a constant
\mathrm{C}_{4}
depending only on
\mu
,
p
, and
\delta
.
Let us fix
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
By Lemma
2.5
, by considering
\phi^{(\epsilon)}
in (
2.5
) instead of
\phi
, it suffices to prove (
3.3
) only for a positive smooth superharmonic function
\phi
in a neighborhood of
\text{supp}(u)
.

Put
c:=-\mu p\in(-p+1,1)
and
v=u\phi^{c/(2p-2)}
.
Using Lemmas
3.4
and
3.3
.(1), and the fact that
(\alpha^{ij})\in\mathrm{M}(\delta,1)
, we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j}\int_{\Omega}\alpha^{ij}u_{ij}|u|^{p-2}u\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(p-1)\sum_{i,j}\int_{\Omega}\alpha^{ij}u_{i}u_{j}|u|^{p-2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x+c\sum_{i,j}\int_{\Omega}\alpha^{ij}|u|^{p-2}uu_{i}\phi_{j}\phi^{c-1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(p-1)\int_{\Omega}\Big(\sum_{i,j}\alpha^{ij}v_{i}v_{j}\Big)|v|^{p-2}\phi^{c^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x-\frac{c^{2}}{4(p-1)}\int_{\Omega}|v|^{p}\Big(\sum_{i,j}\alpha^{ij}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\Big)\phi^{c^{\prime}-2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle(p-1)\delta\int_{\Omega}|\nabla v|^{2}|v|^{p-2}\phi^{c^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x-\frac{c^{2}}{4(p-1)}\int_{\Omega}|v|^{p}|\nabla\phi|^{2}\phi^{c^{\prime}-2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla v|^{2}|v|^{p-2}\phi^{c^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,,

where
c^{\prime}:=\frac{(p-2)c}{2p-2}<1
and

\kappa^{\prime}=\delta(p-1)-\frac{1}{4(p-1)}\big(\frac{pc}{1-c^{\prime}}\big)^{2}\,.

Observe that
\kappa^{\prime}>0
if and only if
\mu:=-\frac{c}{p}
satisfies (
3.4
).
Therefore it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq N(p,\mu)\int_{\Omega}|v|^{p-2}|\nabla v|^{2}\phi^{c^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(3.8)

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|v|^{p-2}|\nabla v|^{2}\phi^{c^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{c}{p-1}\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}u(\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi)\phi^{c-1}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

If
c\in[0,1)
, then because
\Delta\phi\leq 0
on
\mathrm{supp}(u)
, we obtain that

\Delta(\phi^{c})=c(\Delta\phi)\phi^{c-1}-c(1-c)|\nabla\phi|^{2}\phi^{c-1}\leq 0

on
\text{supp}(u)
.
This implies that

\frac{c}{p-1}\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}u(\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi)\phi^{c-1}\,\mathrm{d}x=-\frac{1}{p(p-1)}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\Delta(\phi^{c})\,\mathrm{d}x\geq 0\,.

Therefore (
3.8
) holds.

If
c\in(-p+1,0)
, then Lemma
3.3
.(1) implies

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{c}{p-1}\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}u(\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi)\phi^{c-1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\frac{c}{p-1}\Big(\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\phi^{c-2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle\frac{p-1+c}{(p-1)(1-c)}\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{c}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Since
p-1+c>0
, the proof is complete.

(2) For a fixed
\mathrm{A}=(\alpha^{ij})_{d\times d}\in\mathrm{M}(\delta,1)
, take
\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{M}\big(\sqrt{\delta},1\big)
such that
\mathrm{B}^{2}=\mathrm{A}
.
We denote
u_{\mathrm{B}}(y):=u(\mathrm{B}y)
and
\phi_{\mathrm{B}}(y):=\phi(\mathrm{B}y)
.
Since

\Delta\phi_{\mathrm{B}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\alpha^{ij}\big(D_{ij}\phi\big)(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)\leq 0

in the sense of distributions on
\mathrm{B}^{-1}\Omega:=\{\mathrm{B}^{-1}x\,:\,x\in\Omega\}
,
Lemma
3.3
.(2) implies that for any
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{u\neq 0\}}|u|^{p-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}\mathrm{det(B)}\int_{\mathrm{B}^{-1}\Omega\cap\{u_{\mathrm{B}}\neq 0\}}|u_{\mathrm{B}}|^{p-2}|\nabla u_{\mathrm{B}}|^{2}\phi_{\mathrm{B}}^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,\mu}\,

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}\mathrm{det(B)}\int_{\mathrm{B}^{-1}\Omega}(-\Delta u_{\mathrm{B}})\cdot u_{\mathrm{B}}|u_{\mathrm{B}}|^{p-2}\phi_{\mathrm{B}}^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}\int_{\Omega}\big(-\alpha^{ij}D_{ij}u\big)\cdot u|u|^{p-2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Therefore the proof is complete.
∎

The following theorem is the main result of this section:

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\Omega
be an open set admitting the Hardy inequality (
1.3
) and
\phi
be a positive superharmonic function in
\Omega
, and let
0<\alpha\leq 1
and
0<T<\infty
.
For any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,\nu_{1}/\nu_{2})
,the following estimate holds for every
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega\big)
with
f:=\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u-\mathcal{L}u
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq N\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{2p-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,,\end{split}

(3.9)

where
N=N(p,\mu,\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega),\mathrm{C}_{4})
.

To prove Theorem
3.5
, we use the following elementary fact: for
F\in C^{1}\big([0,T]\big)
with
F(0)=0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}|F(t)|^{p-2}F(t)\partial_{t}^{\alpha}F(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0\,.

(3.10)

If
\alpha=1
, then it follows from a direct calculation, while for
0<\alpha<1
, it follows from Lemma
2.2
.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

By Lemma
3.3
.(3) and the fact that
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\phi,p,\nu_{1}/\nu_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,&\lesssim_{p,\mu,\mathrm{C}_{1}(\Omega)}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}|u|^{p-2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\mathrm{C}_{4}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\Big(-\sum_{i,j}a^{ij}(t)D_{ij}u\Big)u|u|^{p-2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,.\end{aligned}

(3.11)

Integrating

p\,\big(\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u\big)u|u|^{p-2}\,\phi^{-\mu p}-p\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a^{ij}(t)D_{ij}u\cdot u|u|^{p-2}\phi^{-\mu p}=pf\cdot u|u|^{p-2}\phi^{-\mu p}

over
(0,T]\times\Omega
and applying Young’s inequality yield, for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle p\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\Big(-\sum_{i,j}a^{ij}(t)D_{ij}u\Big)u|u|^{p-2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle-p\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\big(\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u\big)u|u|^{p-2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

(3.12)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{-p+1}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{2p-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+(p-1)\epsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-p+1}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{2p-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+(p-1)\epsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

where the last inequality follows from (
3.10
).
Combining (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) and choosing
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, we obtain (
3.9
); note that because
\phi^{-\mu p}
is locally integrable (see Lemmas
2.4
.(2) and (3)), the first term in (
3.11
) is finite.
∎

Recall that
\langle F,\zeta\rangle
denotes the action of
F\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
on
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

Lemma 3.6

(Existence of a very weak solution)
.

Let
\Omega
be an open set admitting the Hardy inequality (
1.3
).
For any
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega\big)
, there exists
u\in L_{\infty}\big([0,T]\times\Omega\big)
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

For any
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T)\big)
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big(I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\langle u(t,\cdot),\zeta\rangle\Big)\eta^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{T}\langle\Delta u(t,\cdot)+f(t,\cdot),\zeta\rangle\eta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

(3.13)

(2)

For any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\mu\in\big(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p}\big)
, and any positive superharmonic function
\phi
in
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq N\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{2p-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s\,,

(3.14)

where
N=N(p,c,\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega))
.

Note that (
3.13
) means that
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=\Delta u+f
with the initial condition
u(0,\cdot)\equiv 0
(see Remark
4.3
.(2)).

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

Take a sequence of infinitely smooth bounded domains
\big(\Omega_{n}\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\text{supp}(f)\subset[0,T]\times\Omega_{1}\,\,,\quad\overline{\Omega_{n}}\subset\Omega_{n+1}\,\,,\quad\bigcup_{n}\Omega_{n}=\Omega

(see,
e.g.
,
[
12
, Proposition 8.2.1]
).
For any
H\in C_{c}^{\infty}((0,T]\times\Omega_{1})
, there exists
U\in C^{\infty}\big([0,T]\times\Omega_{n}\big)\cap C\big([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega_{n}}\big)
satisfying

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}U=\Delta U+H1_{\Omega_{1}}\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\,(0,T]\times\Omega_{n}\quad;\quad U(t,x)=0\quad\text{if}\,\,\,\,t=0\,\,\,\,\text{or}\,\,\,\,x\in\partial\Omega_{n}\,,

(3.15)

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{n}}|U|^{p-1}|D_{x}^{2}U|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\,.

(3.16)

It is a classical result if
\alpha=1
.
For
0<\alpha<1
, the existence of such
U
is established in Lemma
B.1
.
Denote this solution
U
by
R_{n}H
, and note the following properties of
R_{n}H
:

•

From (
3.15
), we deduce that for any
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})
and
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T)\big)
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big(I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\langle R_{n}H(t,\cdot),\zeta\rangle\Big)\eta^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{T}\langle\Delta\big(R_{n}H\big)(t,\cdot)+H(t,\cdot)1_{\Omega_{1}},\zeta\rangle\eta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

(3.17)

•

The maximum principle (for when
0<\alpha<1
, see
[
50
, Theorem 2]
) yields

\displaystyle\big|R_{n}H(t,x)\big|\leq\frac{\|H\|_{\infty}}{\Gamma(1+\alpha)}t^{\alpha}\,.

(3.18)

•

\overline{\Omega}_{n}
is a compact subset of
\Omega
,
R_{n}H\in C([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}_{n})\cap C^{\infty}([0,T]\times\Omega_{n})
, and
R_{n}H\equiv 0
on
\overline{\big((0,T]\times\Omega_{n}\big)}\setminus\big((0,T]\times\Omega_{n}\big)
.
These and (
3.16
) imply that
R_{n}H(t,\,\cdot\,)1_{\Omega_{n}}
satisfies condition (
3.5
).
Repeating the proof of Theorem
3.5
for
(R_{n}H)1_{\Omega_{n}}
in place of
u
and using Lemma
3.3
.(2) instead of (
3.3
), we obtain that for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\mu\in\big(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p}\big)
, and any positive superharmonic function
\phi
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|R_{n}H|^{p}\rho^{-2}\phi^{-\mu p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s\leq N(p,c,\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega))\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|H|^{p}\phi^{-\mu p}\rho^{2p-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s\,.

(3.19)

Take
F\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T]\times\Omega_{1}\big)
such that
|f|\leq F
, and put
f_{1}:=F+f
and
f_{2}:=F
so that
f_{1},\,f_{2}\geq 0
and
f=f^{1}-f^{2}
.

For
v_{n}:=R_{n}\big(f^{1}1_{\Omega_{n}}\big)
, the maximum principle implies that
0\leq v_{n}\leq v_{n+1}
.
We denote the pointwise limit of
v_{n}
by
v
.
In the same manner, we define
w:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}R_{n}\big(f^{2}1_{\Omega_{n}}\big)
.
By (
3.18
),
v
and
w
are bounded.
Put

u:=v-w=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}R_{n}f1_{\Omega_{n}}\,.

it follows from (
3.18
) that
|u(t,x)|\lesssim t^{\alpha}
, which implies that
I^{1-\alpha}\big(|\langle u(t,\cdot),\zeta\rangle|\big)\lesssim t
.
Applying Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem to (
3.17
) and Fatou’s lemma to (
3.19
) yields (
3.13
) and (
3.14
),, respectively.
∎

4.
Solvability of the heat equation

Throughout this section, we assume (
3.1
) and that
\Omega
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

4.1.
Solution spaces

This subsection introduces the solution spaces
\Psi\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma}(\Omega,T)
in the case where the initial datum
u_{0}
in (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) is zero, and
\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma}(\Omega,T)
for general
u_{0}
.

Throughout this subsection, we assume that
\Psi
is a regular Harnack function in
\Omega
, that
0<\alpha\leq 1
,
1<p<\infty
, and
\gamma,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
; we then denote

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)

\displaystyle:=L_{p}\big((0,T);H^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)\,\,,

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)

\displaystyle:=L_{p}\big((0,T);L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)\,\,,

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)

\displaystyle:=L_{p}\big((0,T);H^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)\big)\,\,,

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{L}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)

\displaystyle:=L_{p}\big((0,T);L_{p,\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)\,\,,

\displaystyle\Psi\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)

\displaystyle:=L_{p}\big((0,T);\Psi H^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)\big)\,\,,

\displaystyle\quad\Psi\mathbb{L}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)

\displaystyle:=L_{p}\big((0,T);\Psi L_{p,\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)\,.

Recall the definition of the Riemann-Liouville fractional integral,
I_{t}^{\beta}
, in Definition
2.1
.

Definition 4.1
.

(1)

\Psi\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
denotes the set of all
u\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
such that there exists
f\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)
satisfying the following: for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, and
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T)\big)
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big(I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\langle u(t),\phi\rangle\Big)\eta^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{T}\langle f(t),\phi\rangle\eta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

(4.2)

where

I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\langle u(t),\phi\rangle:=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{(t-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\langle u(s),\phi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\,.

For such
u\in\Psi\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
, we denote
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u:=f
and define

\|u\|_{\Psi\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)}:=\|u\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)}+\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)}\,.

(2)

For
0<\frac{1}{p}<\alpha\leq 1
,
\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
denotes the set of all
u:(0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
such that
u\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
, and there exist
u_{0}\in\Psi B_{p,\theta+2/\alpha}^{\gamma+2-2/(p\alpha)}(\Omega)
and
f\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)
satisy the following: for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\langle u(t),\phi\rangle=\langle u_{0},\phi\rangle+I_{t}^{\alpha}\langle f(t),\phi\rangle\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,\,t\in(0,T]\,.

(4.3)

For such
u\in\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
, we set
u(0):=u_{0}
,
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u:=f
and define

\|u\|_{\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)}:=\|u\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)}+\|u(0)\|_{\Psi B_{p,\theta+2/\alpha}^{\gamma+2-2/(\alpha p)}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)}\,.

We similarly define
\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p}^{\alpha,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
and
\mathcal{H}_{p}^{\alpha,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
in the same manner as
\Psi\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma}(\Omega,T)
and
\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma}(\Omega,T)
, respectively, by replacing the domain
\Omega
with
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the function spaces
\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)
and
\Psi B_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
with
\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
and
B_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, respectively.

Remark 4.2
.

The space
\Psi\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
is designed for the problem with zero initial data in (
3.2
).
The motivation for this definition comes from the identity that, for
F\in C^{1}\big([0,T]\big)
with
F(0)=0
, we have

\partial_{t}^{\alpha}F(t):=I_{t}^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}F(t)=\partial_{t}I_{t}^{1-\alpha}F(t)\,.

Consequently, for any
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T)\big)
,

\int_{0}^{T}\Big(I_{t}^{1-\alpha}F(t)\Big)\eta^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{T}\big(\partial_{t}^{\alpha}F(t)\big)\eta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

Notably, if
\alpha<\frac{1}{p}
, then the problem with nonzero initial data can be reduced to the zero initial data case.
More precisely, let
u\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
,
u_{0}\in\Psi H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, and
f\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, and suppose that they satisfy (
4.3
).
Define

\widetilde{f}(t,\cdot)=\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}u_{0}(\cdot)+f(t,\cdot)\,,

Then
\widetilde{f}\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)
, and
\langle u(t),\phi\rangle=I_{t}^{\alpha}\langle\widetilde{f}(t),\phi\rangle
for all
t\in(0,T]
.
Consequently,
u\in\Psi\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
(see Lemma
4.4
.(1)).

The space
\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
is intended for the problem with nonzero initial data in (
3.2
) when
\alpha>\frac{1}{p}
.
For
\alpha=\frac{1}{p}
,
[
14
]
provides a result for
u_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, under additional assumptions on the regularity of
u_{0}
.
We refer the reader to
[
29
]
for a detailed discussion of
\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
for general
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Remark 4.3
.

(1)

All quantities in (
4.2
) are well-defined.
More precisely,
I_{t}^{1-\alpha}|\langle u(t),\phi\rangle|
and
\langle f(t),\phi\rangle
lie in
L_{p}([0,T])
.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(I_{t}^{1-\alpha}|\langle u(t),\phi\rangle|\Big)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{1/p}&\leq\int_{0}^{T}\frac{s^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\bigg(\int_{s}^{T}|\langle u(t-s),\phi\rangle|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{1/p}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\lesssim\frac{T^{1-\alpha}}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\|u\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)}\|\phi\|_{\Psi^{-1}H_{p^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}^{-\gamma-2}(\Omega)}\,,\\
\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|\langle f(t),\phi\rangle|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{1/p}\,&\lesssim\|f\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)}\|\phi\|_{\Psi^{-1}H_{p^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}-2p^{\prime}}^{-\gamma}(\Omega)}\,,\end{aligned}

(4.4)

where
p^{\prime}
and
\theta^{\prime}
are the constants in (
2.12
) (the first inequality follows from Minkowski’s inequality, whereas the latter two follow from (
2.13
)).

Since
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is a separable topological vector space, identity (
4.2
) implies the uniqueness of
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u:=f\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)
for
u\in\Psi\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
.

(2)

The term
I_{t}^{\alpha}\langle f(t),\phi\rangle
in (
4.3
) is well-defined for all
t\in(0,T]
, as can be seen from the following estimate:
