\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}\mathcal{H}_{p,-2/\alpha-\delta_{1}p}^{1}(\Omega,T)}\lesssim F+I

Since

\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\psi(x)^{-1+\epsilon}\rho(x)^{-\delta_{1}}u(t,x)|\simeq|\widetilde{\psi}^{-1+\epsilon}u(t)|_{0,0}^{(-\delta_{1})}\lesssim\big|\widetilde{\psi}^{-1+\epsilon}u(t)\big|_{0,1-2/(\alpha p)-d/p}^{(-\delta_{1})}\,,

(
5.32
) is proved.
Since
\psi
is bounded, taking
\delta_{1}>0
yields
u(t,x)\to 0
as
(t,x)\to[0,T]\times\partial\Omega
, which means that
u\in C([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega})
and
u|_{[0,T]\times\partial\Omega}\equiv 0
.
∎

5.2.
Domains with thin exterior: Aikawa dimension

Recall the definition of the Aikawa dimension.
For a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Aikawa dimension of
E
, denoted by
\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(E)
, is defined by

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(E)=\inf\Big\{\beta\geq 0\,:\,\sup_{p\in E,\,r>0}\frac{1}{r^{\beta}}\int_{B_{r}(p)}\frac{1}{d(y,E)^{d-\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty\Big\}

(5.37)

with considering
\frac{1}{0}=\infty
.
For more information on the Aikawa dimension, see Remark
5.3
.

The following theorem is given in
[
62
, Theorem 4.17]
:

Theorem 5.12
.

Suppose there exists
\beta<d-2
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{p\in\Omega^{c},\,r>0}\frac{1}{r^{\beta}}\int_{B_{r}(p)}\frac{1}{d(y,\Omega^{c})^{d-\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}y\leq A_{\beta}<\infty\,.

(5.38)

Then there exists a superharmonic function
\phi
in
\Omega
such that

\displaystyle N^{-1}\rho(x)^{-d+2+\beta}\leq\phi(x)\leq N\rho(x)^{-d+2+\beta}\,,

where
N=N(d,\beta,A_{\beta})>0
.

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
\Omega
satisfy
\beta_{0}:=\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(\Omega^{c})<d-2
.
For any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\beta_{0}<\theta<(d-2-\beta)p+\beta_{0}\,,

and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
the assertions in Corollary
5.9
holds for this
\Omega
,
p
,
\theta
, and
\gamma
.
Here,
N
in (
5.9
) and (
5.10
) depend only on
d
,
p
,
\gamma
,
\theta
,
\beta_{0}
,
\{A_{\beta}\}_{\beta>\beta_{0}}
.

Proof of Corollary
5.13
.

By Lemma
5.2
,
\Omega
admits the Hardy inequality (
1.3
), and
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)
can be chosen to depend only on
d
,
\beta_{0}
, and
\{A_{\beta}\}_{\beta>\beta_{0}}
.

Take
\beta\in(\beta_{0},d-2)
such that

\beta<\theta<(d-2-\beta)p+\beta\,.

By Theorem
5.12
, there exists a superharmonic function
\phi
such that
\widetilde{\rho}^{\,-d+2+\beta}
is a regularization of
\phi
, and the constants
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\,-d+2+\beta})
and
\mathrm{C}_{3}(\phi,\widetilde{\rho}^{\,-d+2+\beta})
(in Definition
2.6
.(3)) can be chosen to depend only on
d
,
\beta
, and
A_{\beta}
.
Therefore, by Theorem
4.8
for
\Psi:=\widetilde{\rho}^{\,-d+2+\beta}
(see Proposition
3.2
.(1) and (
2.23
)), the proof is complete.
∎

5.3.
Convex domains

We recall the definition of a convex set.
A set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be
convex
if
(1-t)x+ty\in E
for all
x,y\in E
and
t\in[0,1]
.
Note that, as is well known (see
e.g.

[
62
, Lemma 5.8]
), an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is convex if and only if, for every
p\in\partial\Omega
, there exists a unit vector
e_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\Omega\subset\{x\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p}<0\}=:U_{p}

(5.39)

(see Figure
5.3
below).

e_{p}
F_{p}

Figure 5.3.
e_{p}
in (
5.39
), and
F_{p}
in (
5.44
)

Recall the definitions of
\mathrm{M}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
and
\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
at the beginning of Section
3
.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
\Omega
be a convex domain.
For any
(\alpha^{ij})_{d\times d}\in\bigcup\limits_{0<\nu\leq 1}\mathrm{M}(\nu^{2},1)
,

\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\alpha^{ij}D_{ij}\rho\leq 0

in the sense of distributions.

We temporarily assume Theorem
5.14
in order to prove Corollary
5.15
.

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex domain, and consider the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=\mathcal{L}u+f\quad\text{in}\,\,(0,T]\,,

(5.40)

where
0<\alpha\leq 1
,
0<T\leq\infty
, and
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu,\nu^{-1})
with
0<\nu\leq 1
.
Then for any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle-p-1<\theta-d<-1\,,

and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, the following assertion holds:

(1)

For any
f\in\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta+2p}(\Omega,T)
, equation (
5.40
)
has a unique solution
u
in
\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)}\leq N\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta+2p}(\Omega,T)}\,,

(5.41)

where
N
depends only on
d
,
p
,
\gamma
,
\theta
,
\nu
.

(2)

If
\frac{1}{p}<\alpha
, then for any
u_{0}\in B^{\gamma+2-2/(p\alpha)}_{p,\theta+2/\alpha}(\Omega)
and
f\in\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta+2p}(\Omega,T)
, equation (
5.40
) with
u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}

has a unique solution
u
in
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)}\leq N\left(\|u_{0}\|_{B^{\gamma+2-2/(p\alpha)}_{p,\theta+2/\alpha}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta+2p}(\Omega,T)}\right)\,,

(5.42)

where
N
depends only on
d
,
p
,
\gamma
,
\theta
,
\nu
.

In particular, the domain
\Omega
is not necessarily bounded, and the constants
N
in (
5.41
) and (
5.42
) are independent of
\Omega
.

Proof of Corollary
5.15
.

Since
\Omega
is convex, it follows from Lemma
5.2
, Example
5.6
.(1), and Proposition
5.7
that
\Omega
admits the Hardy inequality (
1.3
), where
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)
can be chosen to depend only on
d
.

Note that
\tilde{\rho}
is a regularization of
\rho
, and the constants
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\tilde{\rho})
and
\mathrm{C}_{3}(\rho,\tilde{\rho})
(in Definition
2.6
) can be chosen to depend only on
d
.
It follows from Proposition
3.2
.(2) that for any
\mu\in(-1/p,1-1/p)
,
\mu
is in
\mathcal{I}(\rho,\nu^{2},p)
.
In addition, the constant
\mathrm{C}_{4}
in (
3.3
) can be chosen to depend only on
\mu
,
p
, and
\nu
.
Put

\mu=-\frac{\theta-d+2}{p}\in\Big(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p}\,\Big)

and apply Theorem
4.8
with
\Psi:=\widetilde{\rho}
(see Proposition
3.2
.(1) and (
2.23
)).
This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.14
.

For each
p\in\partial\Omega
, define
W_{p}(x):=(p-x)\cdot e_{p}
, where
e_{p}
is a unit vector satisfying (
5.39
).
We first claim that

\displaystyle\rho(x)=\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega}W_{p}(x)\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,x\in\Omega\,.

(5.43)

For a fixed
x\in\Omega
, we have

\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega}W_{p}(x)=\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega}d(x,F_{p})\geq\rho(x)\,,

where

\displaystyle F_{p}:=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(y-p)\cdot e_{p}=0\}\subset\Omega^{c}

(5.44)

(see Figure
5.3
above).
For the inverse inequality, take
p_{x}\in\partial\Omega
such that
|x-p_{x}|=\rho(x)
.
Since

B\big(x,\rho(x)\big)\subset\Omega\quad\text{and}\quad p_{x}\in\partial B\big(x,\rho(x)\big)\,,

we obtain that
e_{p_{x}}=\frac{p_{x}-x}{|p_{x}-x|}
.
Therefore

\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega}W_{p}(x)\leq W_{p_{x}}(x)=|p_{x}-x|=\rho(x).

Consequently, (
5.43
) is proved.

Let
\mathrm{A}=(\alpha^{ij})_{d\times d}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu^{2},1)
,
\nu\in(0,1]
, and take
\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu,1)
such that
\mathrm{B}^{2}=\mathrm{A}
.
For any
p\in\partial\Omega
,

\Delta\big(W_{p}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)\big)\equiv 0\quad\text{on}\,\,\mathrm{B}^{-1}\Omega\,.

By (
5.43
) and Lemma
5.1
.(2), we obtain that
\rho(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is an infimum of classical superharmonic functions, and therefore
\rho(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is a superharmonic function.
Consequently, we have

\langle\alpha^{ij}D_{ij}\rho,\zeta\rangle=\det(\mathrm{A})^{1/2}\big\langle\Delta(\rho(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)),\zeta(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)\big\rangle\leq 0

for any
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
\zeta\geq 0
.
∎

5.4.
Exterior Reifenberg condition

The vanishing Reifenberg condition was introduced by Reifenberg
[
60
]
and has since been extensively studied in the literature (see,
e.g.
,
[
7
,
9
,
27
,
64
]
).
The following definition is given in
[
7
,
27
]
:
For
\delta\in(0,1)
and
R>0
, a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to satisfy the
(\delta,R)
-
Reifenberg condition
if for every
p\in\partial\Omega
and
r\in(0,R]
, there exists a unit vector
e_{p,r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Omega\cap B_{r}(p)\subset\{x\in B_{r}(p)\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p,r}<\delta r\}\quad\text{and}\\
&\Omega\cap B_{r}(p)\supset\{x\in B_{r}(p)\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p,r}>-\delta r\}\,.\end{split}

(5.45)

In addition,
\Omega
is said to satisfy the
vanishing Reifenberg condition
if for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists
R_{\delta}>0
such that
\Omega
satisfies the
(\delta,R_{\delta})
-Reifenberg condition.
Note that the vanishing Reifenberg condition is weaker than having
C^{1}
boundary; see Example
5.18
.

In this subsection, we deal with the totally vanishing exterior Reifenberg condition, which was introduced in
[
62
]
.
We obtain a result similar to Corollary
5.15
for domains satisfying the totally vanishing exterior Reifenberg condition (see Corollary
5.20
).

Definition 5.16

(Exterior Reifenberg condition)
.

(1)

\mathbf{ER}_{\Omega}
denotes the set of all
(\delta,R)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying the following: for each
p\in\partial\Omega
, and each connected component
\Omega_{p,R}^{(i)}
of
\Omega\cap B(p,R)
, there exists a unit vector
e_{p,R}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\Omega_{p,R}^{(i)}\subset\{x\in B_{R}(p)\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p,R}^{(i)}<\delta R\}\,.

We denote by
\delta(R):=\delta_{\Omega}(R)
the infimum of all
\delta
such that
(\delta,R)\in\mathbf{ER}_{\Omega}
.

(2)

For
\delta\in[0,1]
,
\Omega
is said to satisfy the
totally
\delta
-exterior Reifenberg condition
(abbreviate to ‘
\langle\mathrm{TER}\rangle_{\delta}
’), if there exist
0<R_{0,\delta}\leq R_{\infty,\delta}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\delta_{\Omega}(R)\leq\delta\quad\text{whenever}\quad R\leq R_{0,\delta}\,\,\,\text{or}\,\,\,R\geq R_{\infty,\delta}\,.

(5.46)

(3)

\Omega
is said to satisfy the
totally vanishing exterior Reifenberg condition
(abbreviate to ‘
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
’), if

\Omega
satisfies
\langle\mathrm{TER}\rangle_{\delta}
condition for all
\delta\in(0,1]
. In other words,

\lim_{R\rightarrow 0}\delta_{\Omega}(R)=\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\delta_{\Omega}(R)=0\,.

For a comparison between the vanishing Reifenberg condition and
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
, see Figure
5.4
and Example
5.18
below.

Remark 5.17
.

As shown in
[
62
, Lemma 5.12]
, for any
R>0
,
\big(\delta(R),R\big)\in\mathbf{ER}_{\Omega}
.

In this subsection, we provide results on domains satisfying
\langle\mathrm{TER}\rangle_{\delta}
for sufficiently small
\delta>0
.
However, our main interest is the condition
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
.

Vanishing
Reifenberg condition
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle^{*}
(Definition
1.4
)
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
(Definition
5.16
)

Figure 5.4.
Totally vanishing exterior Reifenberg condition

Example 5.18

(Example 5.13 in
[
62
]
)
.

(1)

If
\Omega
is bounded, then
\delta(R)\leq\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{R}
, and hence
\lim_{R\to\infty}\delta(R)=0
.

(2)

If
\Omega
satisfies the
(\delta,R_{1})
-Reifenberg condition,
then
\delta(R)\leq\delta
for all
R\leq R_{1}
.
In particular, if
\Omega
satisfies the vanishing Reifenberg condition, then
\lim_{R\rightarrow 0}\delta(R)=0
.

(3)

Suppose that
\Omega
is bounded, and for each
p\in\partial\Omega
there exist
R>0
and
f\in\lambda_{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
such that

\Omega\cap B(p,R)=\big\{y=(y^{\prime},y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,|y|<R\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,y_{n}>f(y^{\prime})\big\}\,,

where
(y^{\prime},y_{n})=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
is an orthonormal coordinate system with origin at
p
, and
\lambda_{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
denotes the little Zygmund class,
i.e.
, the set of all
f\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
such that

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\frac{|f(x+h)-2f(x)+f(x-h)|}{|h|}=0\,.

Then
\Omega
satisfies the vanishing Reifenberg condition, and hence
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
.

(4)

If
\Omega
satisfies the exterior
R_{0}
-ball condition, then
\delta(R)\leq R/(2R_{0})
, and hence
\lim_{R\to 0}\delta(R)=0
.

(5)

If
\Omega
is the intersection of finitely many domains satisfying the totally vanishing Reifenberg condition, then
\Omega
also satisfies
\langle\mathrm{TVER}\rangle
.

A sufficient condition for
\lim_{R\to\infty}\delta_{\Omega}(R)=0
is
\delta_{\Omega}(R)\lesssim 1/R
, and this holds if
\Omega
is bounded.
It was shown in
[
62
, Proposition 5.14]
that, for a general domain
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\Omega}(R)\lesssim 1/R\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\sup_{p\in\partial\Omega}d\big(p,\partial(\Omega_{\mathrm{c.h.}})\big)<\infty\,,

(5.47)

where
\Omega_{\mathrm{c.h.}}
is the convex hull of
\Omega
,
i.e.
,

\Omega_{\mathrm{c.h.}}=\{tx+(1-t)y\,:\,x,\,y\in\Omega\,,\,\,t\in[0,1]\}\,.

We now state the main result of this subsection.
We temporarily assume Theorem
5.19
(its proof will be given at the end of this subsection) in order to prove Corollary
5.20
.

Theorem 5.19
.

For any
\nu\in(0,1]
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists
\delta_{1}>0
depending only on
d,\,\epsilon,\,\nu
such that, if

\Omega
satisfies
\langle\mathrm{TER}\rangle_{\delta_{1}}
,
then there exists a measurable function
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that the following hold:

(1)

For any
(\alpha^{ij})_{d\times d}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu^{2},1)
,
\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\alpha^{ij}D_{ij}\phi\leq 0
in the sense of distributions.

(2)

There exists
N=N(d,\nu,\epsilon,R_{0,\delta}/R_{\infty,\delta})>0
such that

N^{-1}\rho(x)^{1-\epsilon}\leq\phi(x)\leq N\rho(x)^{1-\epsilon}\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,x\in\Omega\,,

where
R_{0,\delta}
and
R_{\infty,\delta}
are the constants in (
5.46
).

Corollary 5.20
.

Consider the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=\mathcal{L}u+f\quad\text{in}\,\,(0,T]\,,

where
0<\alpha\leq 1
,
0<T\leq\infty
, and
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu,\nu^{-1})
with
0<\nu\leq 1
.
Then, for any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle-p-1<\theta-d<-1\,,

there exists
\delta>0
, depending only on
d
,
p
,
\theta
, and
\nu
, such that the following holds:

If
\Omega
satisfies
\langle\mathrm{TER}\rangle_{\delta}
, then the assertions in Corollary
5.15
hold for this
\Omega
,
p
,
\theta
, and any
\gamma>0
.
In addition,
N
in (
5.41
) and (
5.42
) depend only on
d
,
p
,
\gamma
,
\theta
,
\nu
,
R_{0,\delta}/R_{\infty,\delta}
.
Here,
R_{0,\delta}
and
R_{\infty,\delta}
are the constants in (
5.46
).

Proof of Corollary
5.20
.

Take
\epsilon\in(0,1)
such that

-p-1+(p-1)\epsilon<\theta-d<-1-\epsilon\,.

For this
\epsilon
and any
\nu\in(0,1]
, let
\delta_{1}>0
denote the constant in Theorem
5.19
.
It was shown in
[
62
, Theorem 5.17, Lemma 4.10]
that there exists
\delta_{2}>0
, depending only on
d
, such that, if
\Omega
satisfies
\langle\mathrm{TER}\rangle_{\delta}
, then
\Omega
admits the Hardy inequality (
1.3
).
Here,
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)
depends only on
d
,
\delta
, and
R_{0,\delta}/R_{\infty,\delta}
.

Put
\delta=\delta_{1}\wedge\delta_{2}>0
.
Then
\Omega
admits the Hardy inequality.
Let
\phi
be the function in Theorem
5.19
.
By Proposition
3.2
.(2), we obtain that for

\mu:=-\frac{\theta-d+2}{p(1-\epsilon)}\in\Big(-\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{p}\Big)\,,

we have
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\phi,\nu^{2},p)
.
In addition, for this
\mu
,
\mathrm{C}_{4}
in (
3.3
) can be chosen to depend only on
\mu
,
\nu
, and
p
.
Note that
\widetilde{\rho}^{\,1-\epsilon}
is a regularization of
\phi
, and
the constants
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\,1-\epsilon})
and
\mathrm{C}_{3}(\phi,\widetilde{\rho}^{\,1-\epsilon})
(in Definition
2.6
) can be chosen to depend only on
d
,
\epsilon
,
\nu
and
R_{0,\delta}/R_{\infty,\delta}
.
By applying Theorem
4.8
with
\Psi:=\widetilde{\rho}^{,1-\epsilon}
(see Proposition
3.2
.(1) and (
2.23
)), this completes the proof.
∎

To prove Theorem
5.19
, we first state the following lemma:

Lemma 5.21
.

Suppose that
(\delta,R)\in\mathbf{ER}_{\Omega}
.
For any
\nu\in(0,1)
and
p\in\partial\Omega
, there exists a continuous function
w_{p,R}:\Omega\rightarrow(0,1]
satisfying the following:

(1)

For any
\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu,1)
,
w_{p,R}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is a classical superharmonic function in
\mathrm{B}^{-1}\Omega
.

(2)

w_{p,R}=1
on
\{x\in\Omega\,:\,|x-p|>(1-\delta)R\}
.

(3)

w_{p,R}\leq M\delta
in
\Omega\cap B(p,\delta R)
.

Here,
M
depends only on
\nu
and
d
, and in particular, it is independent of
\delta
.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is almost the same as that of
[
62
, Lemma 5.20]
, by setting
F_{0}(t)=1-t^{2-d/\nu^{2}}
instead of
[
62
, (5.21)]
.
We leave the complete proof to the reader.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.19
.

It suffices to consider the case
\nu\in(0,1)
.
Let
M>0
be the constant in Lemma
5.21
.
For a fixed
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, choose
\delta\in(0,1)
sufficiently small such that
M\delta<\delta^{1-\epsilon}
, and then choose
\eta\in(0,1)
sufficiently small such that

(1-\eta)M\delta+\eta\leq\delta^{1-\epsilon}\,.

We assume that
\Omega
satisfies (
5.46
) for this
\delta
.
By scaling and using the fact that
(\delta(R),R)\in\mathbf{ER}_{\Omega}
(see Remark
5.17
), we may assume without loss of generality that
R_{\infty,\delta}=1
, and that
(\delta,R)\in\mathbf{ER}_{\Omega}
whenever
R\leq R_{0,\delta}(\leq 1)
or
R\geq 1
.

Step 1.

Put

k_{0}=\min\big\{k\in\mathbb{N}\,:\,\delta^{k}\leq R_{0,\delta}\big\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{I}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\,:\,k\leq 0\,\,\,\text{or}\,\,\,k\geq k_{0}\}\,,

so that
(\delta,\delta^{k})\in\mathbf{ER}_{\Omega}
for every
k\in\mathcal{I}
.
For each
p\in\partial\Omega
and
k\in\mathcal{I}
, put

\displaystyle\phi_{p,k}=\delta^{k(1-\epsilon)}\Big((1-\eta)\,w_{p,\delta^{k}}+\eta\Big)\,,

where
w_{p,\delta^{k}}
is the function
w_{p,R}
in Lemma
5.21
with
R=\delta^{k}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\qquad\phi_{p,k}(x)\leq\delta^{(k+1)(1-\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad\Omega\cap\overline{B}(p,\delta^{k+1})\,;

\displaystyle\qquad\phi_{p,k}(x)=\delta^{k(1-\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad\Omega\cap\partial B(p,\delta^{k})\,;

\displaystyle\eta\cdot\delta^{k(1-\epsilon)}\leq\phi_{p,k}\leq\delta^{k(1-\epsilon)}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad\Omega\cap B(p,\delta^{k})\,.

Put

\displaystyle\phi_{p}^{(1)}(x):=\inf\{\phi_{p,k}(x)\,:\,k\geq k_{0}\,\,,\,\,|x-p|<\delta^{k}\}\quad

\displaystyle\text{for}\quad|x-p|<\delta^{k_{0}}\,;

\displaystyle\phi_{p}^{(2)}(x):=\inf\{\phi_{p,k}(x)\,:\,k\leq 0\,\,,\,\,|x-p|<\delta^{k}\}\quad

\displaystyle\text{for}\quad|x-p|>\delta\,.

In this step, we first claim that, for any
\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu,1)
,
\phi_{p}^{(1)}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
and
\phi_{p}^{(2)}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
are classical superharmonic functions in

\{\mathrm{B}^{-1}x\,:\,x\in\Omega\cap B(p,\delta^{k_{0}})\}\quad\text{and}\quad\{\mathrm{B}^{-1}x\,:\,x\in\Omega\setminus\overline{B}(p,\delta)\}\,,

respectively.
The second claim is that for each
i=1,\,2
,
\phi_{p}^{(i)}(x)
satisfies

\displaystyle\eta|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}\leq\phi_{p}^{(i)}(x)\leq\delta^{-1+\epsilon}|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}

(5.48)

on its domain.
By similarity, we only provide the proof for
\phi^{(1)}_{p}
.

For the first claim, it suffices to prove that, for each
\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu,1)
and
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k_{1}\geq k_{0}
, the function
\phi_{p}^{(1)}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is a classical superharmonic function in

U:=\{\mathrm{B}^{-1}x\in\Omega\,:\,\delta^{k_{1}+2}<|x-p|<\delta^{k_{1}}\}\,.

For
x\in U
, put

\displaystyle v_{p,k_{1}}(x)=\begin{cases}\phi_{p,k_{1}}(x)&\text{if}\quad\delta^{k_{1}+1}\leq|x-p|<\delta^{k_{1}}\\
\phi_{p,k_{1}}(x)\wedge\phi_{p,k_{1}+1}(x)&\text{if}\quad\delta^{k_{1}+2}<|x-p|<\delta^{k_{1}+1}\,.\end{cases}

Since
\phi_{p,k_{1}}\leq\phi_{p,k_{1}+1}
on
\Omega\cap\partial B(p,\delta^{k_{1}+1})
, Lemma
5.1
.(4) implies that
v_{p,k_{1}}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is a classical superharmonic function in
U
.
Observe that

\phi_{p}^{(1)}(x)=v_{p,k_{1}}(x)\wedge\inf\{\phi_{p,k}(x)\,:\,k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{1}-1\}

on
U
.
If
\eta\,\delta^{k(1-\epsilon)}\geq\delta^{k_{1}(1-\epsilon)}
then

v_{p,k_{1}}(x)\leq\phi_{p,k_{1}}(x)\leq\delta^{k_{1}(1-\epsilon)}\leq\eta\,\delta^{k(1-\epsilon)}\leq\phi_{p,k}(x)\,.

Therefore we have

\displaystyle\phi_{p}^{(1)}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)=v_{p,k_{1}}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)\wedge\inf\{\phi_{p,k}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)\,:\,k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\eta\,\delta^{k(1-\epsilon)}\leq\delta^{k_{1}(1-\epsilon)}\}\,.

This implies that on
U
,
\phi_{p}^{(1)}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is the minimum of finitely many classical superharmonic functions.
It follows from Lemma
5.1
.(1) that
\phi_{p}^{(1)}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is a classical superharmonic function in
U
.

For the second claim, let
x\in\Omega
satisfy
\delta^{k_{1}+1}\leq|x-p|<\delta^{k_{1}}
for
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k_{1}\geq k_{0}
.
Since
\phi^{(1)}_{p,k_{1}}(x)\leq\delta^{k_{1}(1-\epsilon)}
, and

\phi^{(1)}_{p,k}(x)\geq\eta\delta^{k(1-\epsilon)}\geq\eta\delta^{k_{1}(1-\epsilon)}
for all
k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{1}
,
we obtain that
\eta\,\delta^{k_{1}(1-\epsilon)}\leq\phi_{p}^{(1)}(x)\leq\delta^{k_{1}(1-\epsilon)}
.
This implies (
5.48
).

Step 2.

Observe that

\displaystyle\phi_{p}^{(1)}(x)\leq\phi_{p,k_{0}}(x)\leq\delta^{(k_{0}+1)(1-\epsilon)}\quad

\displaystyle\text{if}\,\,\,|x-p|=\delta^{k_{0}+1}\,\,;

(5.49)

\displaystyle\phi_{p}^{(1)}(x)=\phi_{p,k_{0}}(x)=\delta^{k_{0}(1-\epsilon)}\quad

\displaystyle\text{if}\,\,\,|x-p|=\delta^{k_{0}}\,.

Put
\gamma=-\nu^{-2}d+2<0
and take
\alpha_{1},\,\beta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the function

f(t):=\alpha_{1}-\beta_{1}t^{\gamma}

satisfies

\displaystyle f(\delta^{k_{0}+1})=\delta^{(k_{0}+1)(1-\epsilon)}\quad\text{and}\quad f(\delta^{k_{0}})=\delta^{k_{0}(1-\epsilon)}\,.

(5.50)

Since
f(\delta^{k_{0}+1})<f(\delta^{k_{0}})
, we have
\beta_{1}>0
.
Because
\gamma=-\nu^{-2}d+2<0
and
\beta_{1}>0
, we have the following:

•

For any
(\alpha^{ij})_{d\times d}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu^{2},1)
,

\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\alpha_{ij}D_{ij}\Big(f\big(|\cdot-p|\big)\Big)\leq 0\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{p\}\,.

•

f(t)
increases as
t\rightarrow\infty
.
In particular,
f(t)\geq\delta^{(k_{0}+1)(1-\epsilon)}
for all
t\geq\delta^{k_{0}+1}
.

Take
\alpha_{2}>0,\,\beta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}\eta\delta^{1-\epsilon}+\beta_{2}=f(\delta)\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha_{2}\delta^{-1+\epsilon}+\beta_{2}=f(1)\,,

(5.51)

and put
\widetilde{\phi}_{p}^{(2)}:=\alpha_{2}\phi_{p}^{(2)}+\beta_{2}
.
We define

\displaystyle\phi_{p}=\begin{cases}\vskip 2.84526pt\,\,\,\phi_{p}^{(1)}&\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\big\{x\in\Omega\,:\,|x-p|\leq\delta^{k_{0}+1}\big\}\\
\vskip 2.84526pt\,\,\,\phi_{p}^{(1)}\wedge f(|\,\cdot\,-p|)&\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\big\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\delta^{k_{0}+1}<|x-p|<\delta^{k_{0}}\big\}\\
\vskip 2.84526pt\,\,\,f(|\,\cdot\,-p|)&\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\big\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\delta^{k_{0}}\leq|x-p|\leq\delta\big\}\\
\vskip 2.84526pt\,\,\,f(|\,\cdot\,-p|)\wedge\widetilde{\phi}_{p}^{(2)}&\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\big\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\delta<|x-p|<1\big\}\\
\vskip 2.84526pt\,\,\,\widetilde{\phi}_{p}^{(2)}&\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\big\{x\in\Omega\,:\,|x-p|\geq 1\big\}\,.\end{cases}

(5.52)

Observe that

\phi_{p}^{(1)}(x)\leq f(|x-p|)
if
|x-p|=\delta^{k_{0}+1}

and

\phi_{p}^{(1)}(x)\geq f(|x-p|)
if
|x-p|=\delta^{k_{0}}

(see (
5.49
) and (
5.50
)), and

f(|x-p|)\leq\widetilde{\phi}_{p}^{(2)}
if
|x-p|=\delta

and

f(|x-p|)\geq\widetilde{\phi}_{p}^{(2)}
if
|x-p|=1

(see (
5.48
) and (
5.51
)).
By Lemma
5.1
.(4), it follows that for any
\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu,1)
,
\widetilde{\phi}_{p}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is a classical superharmonic function in
\mathrm{B}^{-1}\Omega
.

Step 3.

We claim that for every
x\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle N^{-1}|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}\leq\phi_{p}(x)\leq N|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}\,,

(5.53)

where
N=N(d,\epsilon,\nu,R_{0,\delta})>0
.
Recall the definition of
\phi_{p}
in (
5.52
).

Step 3.1)
By (
5.48
),

\displaystyle\eta|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}\leq\phi_{p}^{(1)}(x)\leq\delta^{-1+\epsilon}|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}\quad\text{on}\quad\big\{x\in\Omega\,:\,|x-p|<\delta^{k_{0}}\big\}\,.

(5.54)

Step 3.2)
Since
f(t)=\alpha_{1}-\beta_{1}t^{\gamma}
,
\beta_{1}>0
, and
\gamma<-d+2\leq 0
,
we have

\displaystyle\delta^{(k_{0}+1)(1-\epsilon)}=f(\delta^{k_{0}+1})\leq f\big(|x-p|\big)\leq f(1)\quad\text{if}\quad\delta^{k_{0}+1}<|x-p|<1\,.

(5.55)

Step 3.3)
Note that
\widetilde{\phi}_{p}^{(2)}=\alpha_{2}\phi_{p}^{(2)}+\beta_{2}
and
\alpha_{2}>0
.
Take
K\geq 1
such that

\alpha_{2}\eta K^{1-\epsilon}\geq 2|\beta_{2}|\,.

For
x\in\Omega
satisfying
\delta<|x-p|<K
, it follows from (
5.48
), (
5.51
), and (
5.55
) that

\displaystyle\delta^{(k_{0}+1)(1-\epsilon)}\leq f(\delta)=\alpha_{2}\eta\delta^{1-\epsilon}+\beta_{2}\leq\widetilde{\phi}_{p}^{(2)}(x)\leq\alpha_{2}\delta^{-1+\epsilon}K^{1-\epsilon}+\beta_{2}\,.

(5.56)

If
|x-p|\geq K
, then

2|\beta_{2}|\leq\alpha_{2}\eta K^{1-\epsilon}\leq\alpha_{2}\eta|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}\,.

By (
5.48
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\eta\alpha_{2}}{2}|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}\leq\widetilde{\phi}_{p}^{(2)}(x)\leq\alpha_{2}\big(\delta^{-1+\epsilon}+\frac{\eta}{2}\,\big)|x-p|^{1-\epsilon}\,.

(5.57)

Since
k_{0},\eta,\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{1},\,\alpha_{2},\,\beta_{2},\,K
depend only on
d,\,\nu,\,\epsilon,\,\delta,\,R_{0,\delta}
,
(
5.52
)–(
5.57
) imply (
5.53
).

Step 4.

Put
\phi(x):=\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega}\phi_{p}(x)
.
Then

N^{-1}\rho(x)\leq\phi(x)\leq N\rho(x)\,,

where
N
is the same constant as in (
5.53
).
For any fixed
\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu,1)
, because
\phi_{p}(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is a classical superharmonic function in
\mathrm{B}^{-1}\Omega
, Lemma
5.1
.(2) yields that
\phi(\mathrm{B}\,\cdot\,)
is superharmonic on
\mathrm{B}^{-1}\Omega
.
∎

5.5.
Conic domains

\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
denotes the
(d-1)
-dimensional unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, that is,
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,|x|=1\}
.
We denote by
A_{\mathbb{S}}
the surface measure on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
Note that for any nonnegative Borel function
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}F(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F(r\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}A_{\mathbb{S}}(\sigma)\right)r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r\,.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a connected and relatively open subset of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and define

\displaystyle\Omega=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\,:\,\frac{x}{|x|}\in\mathcal{M}\big\}

which is the conic domain generated by
\mathcal{M}
(see Figure
5.5
below).

Figure 5.5.
Conic domains

We denote

B_{R}^{\Omega}:=\Omega\cap B_{R}(0)\quad\text{and}\quad Q_{R}^{\Omega}:=(1-R^{2},1]\times B_{R}^{\Omega}\,.

In this subsection, for a certain class of sets
\mathcal{M}
including Lipschitz cones (see Assumption
5.23
), we prove that if
u
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}u_{t}=\Delta u\quad&\text{in}\quad Q_{1}^{\Omega}\,;\\
u=0\quad&\text{on}\quad(0,1]\times\big((\partial\Omega)\cap B_{1}(0)\big)\,,\end{cases}

then for any
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0})
and
0<R<1
,

\displaystyle|u(t,x)|\lesssim_{\mathcal{M},\lambda,R}|x|^{\lambda}\sup_{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}|u|\quad\text{whenever}\quad(t,x)\in Q_{R}^{\Omega}

(5.58)

(see Remark
5.26
), where
\lambda_{0}
is the constant defined in (
5.66
).

Remark 5.22
.

As shown in
[
37
]
, estimate (
5.58
) is closely related to heat kernel estimates.
In
[
37
, Lemma 3.9]
, Kozlov and Nazarov used an estimate of the form (
5.58
) to derive bounds for the kernel of parabolic equations in
C^{1,1}
cones.

Before stating the main result of this subsection (Theorem
5.25
), we introduce the notions of the spherical gradient and the spherical Laplacian, formulated without differential geometric terminology.
For a function
f
on
\mathcal{M}
, we denote
F_{f}(x):=f\big(\frac{x}{|x|}\big)
.
We also denote

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle:=\text{the set of all $f:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$ for which $F_{f}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)$}\,;

\displaystyle C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle:=\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\,:\,\mathrm{supp}(f)\subset\mathcal{M}\}\,.

The spherical gradient and spherical Laplacian of
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, denoted by
\nabla_{\mathbb{S}}f
and
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}f
, are defined by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbb{S}}f=\nabla F_{f}|_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}f=\Delta F_{f}|_{\mathcal{M}}\,.

Direct calculation shows that:

•

For any
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
and
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}}\big(\nabla_{\mathbb{S}}f,\nabla_{\mathbb{S}}g\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{\mathbb{S}}=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}f)g\,\,\mathrm{d}A_{\mathbb{S}}\,,

where
(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is the inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

•

For any
F\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle|\nabla F|^{2}=|D_{r}F|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{S}}F|^{2}\,.

(5.59)

•

For a function
F\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\Delta F=D_{rr}F+\frac{d-1}{r}D_{r}F+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}F\,.

(5.60)

In (
5.59
) and (
5.60
),
F
is also viewed as a function defined on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}
, given by
(r,\sigma)\mapsto F(r\sigma)
.
We leave it to the reader to verify that
\nabla_{\mathbb{S}}
(resp.
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}
) coincides with the gradient (resp. Laplace–Beltrami operator) associated with the standard differential structure on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
; see
[
26
]
for details.

We make certain assumptions on
\mathcal{M}
in order to apply Theorem
5.11
.(2).

Assumption 5.23
.

Define
\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\mathcal{M}
.

(1)

\mathcal{M}
is a connected (relatively) open set in
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with
\overline{\mathcal{M}}\neq\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

(2)

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M}\\
r\in(0,1]\end{subarray}}\frac{A_{\mathbb{S}}\big(\{\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\setminus\mathcal{M}\,:\,|\sigma-p|<r\}\big)}{r^{d-1}}>0\,,

(5.61)

(3)

Let
w_{0}(\sigma)
be the first (positive) Dirichlet eigenfunction of the spherical Laplacian
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}
on
\mathcal{M}
(see Proposition
5.24
.(1)).
There exist constants
A,\,N>0
such that

\displaystyle w_{0}(\sigma)\geq N^{-1}d(\sigma,\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M})^{A}\,.

(5.62)

By
\mathring{W}_{2}^{1}(\mathcal{M})
, we denote the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
in

W_{2}^{1}(\mathcal{M}):=\{f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M})\,:\,\|f\|_{L_{2}(\mathcal{M})}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{S}}f\|_{L_{2}(\mathcal{M})}<\infty\}\,.

Proposition 5.24
.

(1)

If Assumption
5.23
.(1) holds, then

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\\
w\not\equiv 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\int_{\mathcal{M}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{S}}w|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{\mathbb{S}}}{\int_{\mathcal{M}}|w|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{\mathbb{S}}}>0\,,

(5.63)

and there exists a unique
w_{0}
in
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\cap\mathring{W}_{2}^{1}(\mathcal{M})
such that

\displaystyle w_{0}>0\quad,\quad\int_{\mathcal{M}}|w_{0}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{\mathbb{S}}=1\quad,\quad\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}w_{0}+\Lambda_{0}w_{0}=0\,.

(5.64)

Moreover,
w_{0}
is bounded on
\mathcal{M}
, and the function

\displaystyle W_{0}(x):=|x|^{\lambda_{0}}w_{0}(x/|x|)

(5.65)

is a positive harmonic function in
\Omega
, where

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}:=-\frac{d-2}{2}+\sqrt{\Lambda_{0}+\Big(\frac{d-2}{2}\Big)^{2}}>0\,.

(5.66)

(2)

If Assumption
5.23
.(2) holds, then

\displaystyle\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega,\,r>0}\frac{\big|\Omega^{c}\cap B_{r}(p)\big|}{\big|B_{r}(p)\big|}>0\,.

(5.67)

(3)

Let
-e_{d}\notin\overline{\mathcal{M}}
and let
\phi_{d}
be the stereographic projection from
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\setminus\{-e_{d}\}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
defined by

\displaystyle\phi_{d}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1},\sigma_{d})=\Big(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{1+\sigma_{d}},\ldots,\frac{\sigma_{d-1}}{1+\sigma_{d}}\Big)\,.

(5.68)

If
\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})
is a John domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
(see Remark
2.9
for its definition), then Assumption
5.23
.(3) holds.

Proof.

(1) (
5.63
) follows from
[
20
, Theorems 10.13, 10.18, 10.22]
.
It is proved in
[
20
, Theorem 10.11, Corollary 10.12]
that there exists a unique
w_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\cap\mathring{W}_{2}^{1}(\mathcal{M})
satisfying (
5.64
).

To prove the boundedness of
w_{0}
, without loss of generality, we assume that
-e_{d}:=(0,\ldots,0,-1)\notin\overline{\mathcal{M}}
.
We denote by
\phi_{d}
the stereographic projection from
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\setminus\{-e_{d}\}
onto
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
defined by (
5.68
).
Then
\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})
is a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.
Consider the function
\widetilde{w}_{0}:=w_{0}\circ\phi_{d}^{-1}:\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
Then
\widetilde{w}_{0}
belongs to
\mathring{W}_{2}^{1}\big(\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})\big)
and satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{d-1}a^{ij}D_{ij}\widetilde{w}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}b^{i}D_{i}\widetilde{w}_{0}+\Lambda_{0}\widetilde{w}_{0}=0\quad\text{in}\quad\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\,,

(5.69)

where
a^{ij},\,b^{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
(
i,\,j=1,\,\ldots,\,d-1
) are smooth functions in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
such that there exists
\nu>0
satisfying

\nu|\xi|^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{d-1}a^{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\nu^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\qquad\forall\,\,\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\,\,,\,\,x\in\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})\,.

The boundedness of
\widetilde{w}_{0}
follows from classical results for elliptic equations (see,
e.g.
,
[
44
, Theorem 3.13.1]
), and hence
w_{0}
is bounded.

It follows directly from (
5.60
) that the function
W_{0}
in (
5.65
) is harmonic in
\Omega
.

(2)
For any
p\in\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M}
and
r\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\big\{s\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,s\in(1-r/2,1+r/2),\,\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap B_{r/2}(p)\big\}

\displaystyle\subset\,

\displaystyle B_{r}(p)\subset\big\{s\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,s\in(1-r,1+r),\,\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap B_{2r}(p)\big\}\,.

Therefore (
5.61
) holds if and only if

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M}\\
r\in(0,1]\end{subarray}}\frac{\big|\Omega^{c}\cap B_{r}(p)\big|}{\big|B_{r}(p)\big|}>0\,.

(5.70)

If
r\geq 2
, then
B_{r}(p)\supset B_{r/2}(0)
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\inf_{p\in\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M},\,r\geq 2}\frac{\big|\Omega^{c}\cap B_{r}(p)\big|}{\big|B_{r}(p)\big|}\geq 2^{-d}\inf_{r\geq 1}\frac{\big|\Omega^{c}\cap B_{r}(0)\big|}{\big|B_{r}(0)\big|}=2^{-d}\frac{A_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\setminus\mathcal{M}\right)}{A_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}>0\,.

(5.71)

Consequently, (
5.67
) is implied by (
5.70
) and (
5.71
).

(3) We denote
U_{\mathcal{M}}:=\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})
.
It follows from Example
2.7
.(2) that
W_{0}
(in (
5.65
)) is a Harnack function in
\Omega
.
Since
W_{0}
is a continuous Harnack function, and
\phi_{d}
(resp.
\phi_{d}^{-1}
) is Lipschitz continuous on
\mathcal{M}
(resp.
U_{\mathcal{M}}
), we obtain that
\widetilde{w}_{0}:=w_{0}\circ\phi_{d}^{-1}
is a continuous Harnack function in
U_{\mathcal{M}}
(see (
2.7
)).
In addition,
d(\sigma,\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M})\simeq d\big(\phi_{d}(\sigma),\partial U_{\mathcal{M}}\big)
.
By Remark
2.9
, since
\Omega
is a John domain, there exists a constant
A>0
such that
\widetilde{w}_{0}(x^{\prime})\gtrsim d(x^{\prime},\partial U_{\mathcal{M}})^{A}
for all
x^{\prime}\in\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})
.
Thus, (
5.62
) is proved.
∎

Theorem 5.25
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
(
d\geq 2
) satisfy Assumption
5.23
, and suppose that
u\in C^{\infty}(Q_{1}^{\Omega})
satisfies

\displaystyle u_{t}=\Delta u\quad\text{in}\quad Q_{1}^{\Omega}\quad;\quad\lim_{(t,x)\rightarrow(t_{0},x_{0})}u(t,x)=0\,\,\,\,\text{whenever}\,\,\,\,0<t_{0}\leq 1\,\,,\,\,x_{0}\in(\partial\Omega)\cap B_{1}\,.

Then for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
R\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle|u(t,x)|\leq N\Big(\sup_{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}|u|\Big)W_{0}(x)^{1-\epsilon}\qquad\forall\,\,(t,x)\in Q_{R}^{\Omega}\,,

where
W_{0}
is the function defined in (
5.65
) and
N=N(\mathcal{M},\epsilon,R)>0
.

Recall that
\mathring{W}_{2}^{1}\big(B_{1}^{\Omega}\big)
is the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}\big(B_{1}^{\Omega}\big)
in
W_{2}^{1}\big(B_{1}^{\Omega}\big)
.

Remark 5.26
.

Theorem
5.25
implies that if
u
satisfies the assumptions of the theorem and
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0})
, where
\lambda_{0}
is given in (
5.66
), then

\displaystyle|u(t,x)|\leq N(\mathcal{M},\lambda,R)\Big(\sup_{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}|u|\Big)|x|^{\lambda}\quad\text{on}\,\,\,Q_{R}^{\Omega}\,.

(5.72)

This holds because
w_{0}
in (
5.65
) is bounded.

It is important to note that for
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
, estimate (
5.72
) does not hold in general.
Indeed, observe that
u(t,x):=W_{0}(x)
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
5.25
.
Given the definition of
W_{0}(x)
in (
5.65
), there is no constant
N
such that (
5.72
) holds with
u(t,x)=W_{0}(x)
and
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
.
To complete this claim, we need to show that
w_{0}
in Proposition
5.24
.(1) satisfies
w_{0}\in C(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
and
w_{0}|_{\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M}}\equiv 0
.
To do this, it suffices to prove that the function
\widetilde{w}_{0}
in the proof of Proposition
5.24
.(1) belongs to
C\big(\overline{\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})}\big)
and
\widetilde{w}_{0}|_{\partial\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})}=0
, assuming, without loss of generality, that
-e_{d}\notin\overline{\mathcal{M}}
.
Assumption
5.23
implies that
\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})
satisfies the volume density condition (
5.7
) in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.
Since
\widetilde{w}_{0}\in\mathring{W}_{2}^{1}\big(\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})\big)
satisfies equation (
5.69
), it follows from classical results for elliptic equations (see,
e.g.
,
[
44
, Theorem 3.14.1]
) that
\widetilde{w}_{0}\in C\big(\overline{\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})}\big)
and
\widetilde{w}_{0}|_{\partial\phi_{d}(\mathcal{M})}=0
.

Proof of Theorem
5.25
.

Step 1.

Put
K=\max(A,\lambda_{0})
where
A
and
\lambda_{0}
are the constants in (
5.62
) and (
5.66
), respectively.
A direct calculation (see,
e.g.
,
[
34
, Lemma 3.4.(1)]
) shows that for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle d(\sigma,\partial\Omega)\leq d(\sigma,\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M})\leq 2d(\sigma,\partial\Omega)\,.

Therefore, we obtain that for any
x\in\Omega\cap B_{1}(0)
,

d(x,\partial B_{1}^{\Omega})^{K}\leq d(x,\partial\Omega)^{K}\simeq|x|^{K}d\big(x/|x|,\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M}\big)^{K}\leq|x|^{\lambda_{0}}d(x/|x|,\partial_{\mathbb{S}}\mathcal{M})^{A}\lesssim W_{0}(x)\,.

Put
\epsilon_{i}=\epsilon+\frac{i}{2K}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and take
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\epsilon_{i_{0}}<1\leq\epsilon_{i_{0}+1}
.
Since
W_{0}
is bounded on
B_{1}^{\Omega}
(see Proposition
5.24
.(1)), we have

\sup_{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i_{0}+1}}u|\lesssim_{\Omega,\epsilon}\sup_{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}|u|\,.

Hence, it remains to prove that for any
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,i_{0}\}
and
0<R_{1}<R_{2}\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{R_{1}}^{\Omega}}|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i}}u|\lesssim N(\Omega,\epsilon,R,r)\sup_{Q_{R_{2}}^{\Omega}}|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i+1}}u|\,.

(5.73)

Step 2.

Take
\eta\in C^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
such that

\displaystyle\eta(t,x)=\begin{cases}\vskip 1.42262pt1\quad&\text{if}\quad t>1-R_{1}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|x|<R_{1}\,;\\
0\quad&\text{if}\quad t<1-R_{2}^{2}\quad\text{or}\quad|x|>R_{2}\,.\end{cases}

Put

\displaystyle v=u\eta\,\,,\,\,\,\,f^{0}:=\big(\partial_{t}\eta+\Delta\eta\big)u\,\,,\,\,\,\,f^{i}:=-2uD_{i}\eta\quad(i=1,\,\ldots,\,d)\,,

(5.74)

so that
v\in C\big(\overline{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}\big)\cap C^{\infty}(Q_{1}^{\Omega})
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v=\Delta v+f_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}\quad\text{in}\quad Q_{1}^{\Omega}\quad;\quad v\equiv 0\quad\text{on}\quad\overline{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}\setminus Q_{1}^{\Omega}\,.

We recall that by Propositions
5.24
.(2) and
5.7
and (
5.7
),
B_{1}^{\Omega}
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in(0,1)
.
Therefore, the results in Section
5.1
are applicable to
B_{1}^{\Omega}
.

Step 2.1)
We first show that
v\in\mathcal{H}_{2,d-2}^{1}(B_{1}^{\Omega},1)
.
Since

\displaystyle\Big\|f^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}_{2,d+2}^{-1}(B_{1}^{\Omega},1)}\lesssim\left\|f^{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2,d+2}(B_{1}^{\Omega},1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|f^{i}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2,d}(B_{1}^{\Omega},1)}\lesssim\sup_{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}|u|

(see (
2.28
) and (
2.10
)),
there exists
\widetilde{v}\in\mathcal{H}_{2,d-2}^{1}(B_{1}^{\Omega},1)
such that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{v}=\Delta\widetilde{v}+f^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{v}(0,\,\cdot\,)=0\,.

(5.75)

Since
f^{0},f^{i}\in C^{\infty}(Q_{1}^{\Omega})
, we obtain that
\widetilde{v}\in C^{\infty}(Q_{1}^{\Omega})
.
Moreover, because
f^{0},\,f^{i}\in C(\overline{Q_{1}^{\Omega}})
, Theorems
5.11
and
4.13
yield that
\widetilde{v}\in C(\overline{Q_{1}^{\Omega}})
and that
v=0
on
\overline{Q_{1}^{\Omega}}\setminus Q_{1}^{\Omega}
.
The maximum principle implies that
v\equiv\widetilde{v}
, and therefore
v\in\mathcal{H}_{2,d-2}^{1}(B_{1}^{\Omega},1)
.

Step 2.2)

To prove (
5.73
), suppose that RHS in (
5.73
) is finite.
Recall that
B_{1}^{\Omega}
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
In addition,
v\in\mathcal{H}_{2,d-2}^{1}(\Omega,1)
(in (
5.74
)) is a solution of equation (
5.75
).
Since
W_{0}
is a regular Harnack function (see Example
2.7
.(2)), and

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{R_{2}}^{\Omega}}\Big(\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i}}d(\cdot,\partial B_{1}^{\Omega})^{2-\lambda\epsilon}f\big|+\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i}}d(\cdot,\partial B_{1}^{\Omega})^{1-\lambda\epsilon}f^{i}\big|\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{N}\,

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{R_{2}}^{\Omega}}\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i}}d(\cdot,\partial B_{1}^{\Omega})^{1-\lambda\epsilon}u\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{N}\,

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{R_{2}}^{\Omega}}\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i}+1/(2K)}u\big|=\sup_{Q_{R_{2}}^{\Omega}}\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i+1}}u\big|\,,

where
N=N(R_{1},R_{2},\mathcal{M},\epsilon)
.
Note that
1-\lambda\epsilon\geq\frac{1}{2}
, so
d(\cdot,\partial B_{1}^{\Omega})^{1-\lambda\epsilon}\leq d(\cdot,\partial B_{1}^{\Omega})^{1/2}
.
Theorem
5.11
.(2) (with Theorem
4.13
) implies that
v
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sup_{(0,1]\times\Omega}\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i}}v|\,&\lesssim\sup_{Q_{R_{2}}^{\Omega}}\Big(\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i}}d(\cdot,\partial B_{1}^{\Omega})^{2-\lambda\epsilon}f\big|+\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i}}d(\cdot,\partial B_{1}^{\Omega})^{1-\lambda\epsilon}f^{i}\big|\Big)\\
&\lesssim\sup_{Q_{R_{2}}^{\Omega}}\big|W_{0}^{-1+\epsilon_{i+1}}u\big|\,.\end{aligned}

(5.76)

Since
v\equiv u
in
Q_{R_{1}}^{\Omega}
, (
5.76
) implies (
5.73
).
Hence, the claim is proved.
∎

Appendix A
Weighted Besov space

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\Phi
be a regular Harnack function, and let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
s\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\Phi f\|_{B_{p,\theta}^{k+s}}^{p}\simeq_{N}\,&\sum_{i=0}^{k}\int_{\Omega}|\rho^{i}D_{x}^{i}f|^{p}\Phi^{p}\rho^{\theta-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\,\,+\int_{\Omega}\bigg(\int_{|x-y|<\frac{\rho(x)}{2}}\frac{|D^{k}_{x}f(x)-D^{k}_{x}f(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}dy\bigg)\Phi(x)^{p}\rho(x)^{(k+s)p+\theta-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\end{aligned}

(A.1)

where
N=N(d,p,k,s,\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Phi))
.

Proof.

Step 1.
We first show that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B_{p,\theta}^{s}(\Omega)}^{p}\simeq\,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}+\int_{\Omega}\int_{|x-y|\leq\frac{\rho(x)}{2}}\frac{|(\widetilde{\rho}^{(\theta-d)/p+s}f)(x)-(\widetilde{\rho}^{(\theta-d)/p+s}f)(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

(A.2)

We recall the following equivalent norm for Besov spaces:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B_{p}^{s}}^{p}\simeq_{d,p,s}\|f\|_{L_{p}}^{p}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

(A.3)

(see,
e.g.
,
[
66
, Theorem 2.5.7/(i)]
).
Recall that for
\xi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we denote
\xi_{(n)}(x):=\xi(\mathrm{e}^{-n}\widetilde{\rho}(x))
.
Applying (
A.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B_{p,\theta}^{s}(\Omega)}^{p}

\displaystyle\simeq_{N}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta}\big\|\big(\zeta_{0,(n)}f\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}\,\cdot\,)\big\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{n\theta}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\big|\big(\zeta_{0,(n)}f\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}x)-\big(\zeta_{0,(n)}f\big)(\mathrm{e}^{n}y)\big|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle I_{1}+I_{2}\,.

(
2.10
) implies that
I_{1}\simeq_{d,p,\theta}\|f\|_{L_{p,\theta}}^{p}
.
By a change of variables, setting
F=\widetilde{\rho}^{\,(\theta-d)/p+s}f
and
\eta(t)=t^{-(\theta-d)/p-s}\zeta_{0}(t)
, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{2}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\zeta_{0,(n)}(x)f(x)-\zeta_{0,(n)}(y)f(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{e}^{n(\theta-d+sp)}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\eta_{(n)}(x)F(x)-\eta_{(n)}(y)F(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\left\{|x-y|\geq\rho(x)/2\right\}}\frac{|\eta_{(n)}(x)F(x)|^{p}+|\eta_{(n)}(y)F(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\left\{|x-y|\leq\rho(x)/2\right\}}\frac{|\eta_{(n)}(x)-\eta_{(n)}(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}|F(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\left\{|x-y|\leq\rho(x)/2\right\}}|\eta_{(n)}(y)|^{p}\frac{|F(x)-F(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle I_{2,1}+I_{2,2}+I_{2,3}\,.

We now estimate each term
I_{2,1}
,
I_{2,2}
, and
I_{2,3}
.
Observe that

\displaystyle N^{-1}\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|\eta(\mathrm{e}^{-n}t)|^{p}\leq N\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-np}|\eta^{\prime}(\mathrm{e}^{-n}t)|^{p}\leq Nt^{-p}\,,

(A.4)

for any
t>0
, where
N=N(d,p,\theta,s)
.
It follows from (
A.4
) that

\displaystyle I_{2,1}\simeq_{N}\int_{\Omega}\int_{y:|x-y|\geq\frac{\rho(x)}{2}}\frac{|F(x)|^{p}+|F(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\simeq_{N}\int_{\Omega}|f(x)|^{p}\rho(x)^{\theta-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\,,

(A.5)

where
N=N(d,p,\theta,s)
; for the last inequality, note that if
|x-y|\geq\frac{\rho(x)}{2}
, then
|x-y|\geq\frac{\rho(y)}{3}
.
To estimate
I_{2,2}
, observe that for
x,\,y\in\Omega
with
|x-y|<\frac{\rho(x)}{2}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|\eta_{(n)}(x)-\eta_{(n)}(y)|^{p}\lesssim_{N}\sum_{n}|x-y|^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-np}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}|\eta^{\prime}(\mathrm{e}^{-n}\widetilde{\rho}(x_{r}))|\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{p}\\
\leq\,\,\,\,&|x-y|^{p}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{n}\mathrm{e}^{-np}|\eta^{\prime}(\mathrm{e}^{-n}\widetilde{\rho}(x_{r}))|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\lesssim_{N}|x-y|^{p}\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{\rho}(x_{r})^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}r\,,\end{split}

(A.6)

where
x_{r}=(1-r)x+ry
and
N=N(d,p,\theta,s)
.
Here, the first inequality follows from the fact that
|\nabla\widetilde{\rho}|
is bounded in
\Omega
, and the last inequality follows from (
A.4
).
Since
\rho(x_{r})\geq\frac{\rho(x)}{2}
, we have

\sum_{n}|\eta_{(n)}(x)-\eta_{(n)}(y)|^{p}\lesssim_{N}\,|x-y|^{p}\rho(x)^{-p}\,,

where
N=N(d,p,\theta,s)
.
Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{2,2}\,

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\Omega}\int_{y:|x-y|\leq\frac{\rho(x)}{2}}\frac{|F(x)|^{p}\rho(x)^{-p}}{|x-y|^{d-(1-s)p}}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim_{d,s,p}\int|f(x)|^{p}\rho(x)^{\theta-d}\,\mathrm{d}x.

(A.7)

By (
A.5
)-(
A.7
) and that
I_{2,3}\lesssim I_{2}+I_{2,2}\lesssim\|f\|_{B_{p,\theta}^{s}}^{p}
,
we have
\|f\|_{B_{p,\theta}^{s}(\Omega)}^{p}\simeq\|f\|_{L_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}+I_{2,3}
.
By applying (
A.4
) to
I_{2,3}
, we conclude that (
A.2
) holds.

Step 2.

We now prove (
A.1
) for the case
k=0
.
Denote
F:=\widetilde{\rho}^{(\theta-d)/p+s}f
.
Since
\Phi\cdot\widetilde{\rho}^{(\theta-d)/p+s}
is a regular Harnack function, if
|x-y|<\rho(x)/2
, then

\displaystyle\Big|\big|\Phi(x)F(x)-\Phi(y)F(y)\big|-\Phi(x)\widetilde{\rho}(x)^{(\theta-d)/p+s}\big|f(x)-f(y)\big|\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\big|\Phi(x)\widetilde{\rho}(x)^{(\theta-d)/p+s}-\Phi(y)\widetilde{\rho}(y)^{(\theta-d)/p+s}\big||f(y)|

(A.8)

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle N|x-y|\cdot\Phi(y)\rho(y)^{-1}|F(y)|

where
N=N(d,\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Phi))
.
By combining (
A.2
) (applied to
\Psi F
instead of
f
), (
A.8
), together with the fact that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{y:|x-y|<\rho(y)}\frac{\big(|x-y|\cdot\Phi(y)\rho(y)^{-1}|F(y)|\big)^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\int_{\Omega}|f(y)|^{p}\Phi(y)^{p}\rho(y)^{\theta-d}\,\mathrm{d}y\,,

we conclude that (
A.1
) holds for
k=0
.

Step 3.
Let
k\geq 1
.
It follows from (
2.24
) that

\|\Phi f\|_{B_{p,\theta}^{k+s}(\Omega)}\simeq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|\Phi D_{x}^{i}f\|_{B_{p,\theta+ip}^{s}(\Omega)}+\|\Phi D_{x}^{k}f\|_{B_{p,\theta+kp}^{s}(\Omega)}\,.

By (
2.20
) and (
2.24
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|\Phi D_{x}^{i}f\|_{L_{p,\theta+ip}(\Omega)}\lesssim\,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|\Phi D_{x}^{i}f\|_{B_{p,\theta+ip}^{s}(\Omega)}\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|\Phi D_{x}^{i}f\|_{H_{p,\theta+ip}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\simeq\,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k}\|\Phi D_{x}^{i}f\|_{L_{p,\theta+ip}(\Omega)}\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|\Phi D_{x}^{i}f\|_{L_{p,\theta+ip}(\Omega)}+\|\Psi D_{x}^{k}f\|_{B_{p,\theta+kp}^{s}(\Omega)}\,.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\|\Phi f\|_{B_{p,\theta}^{k+s}(\Omega)}\simeq\sum_{i=0}^{k}\|\Phi D_{x}^{i}f\|_{B_{p,\theta+ip}^{s}(\Omega)}\simeq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|\Phi D_{x}^{i}f\|_{L_{p,\theta+ip}(\Omega)}+\|\Psi D_{x}^{k}f\|_{B_{p,\theta+kp}^{s}(\Omega)}\,.

By Lemma
2.11
and Step 2 ((
A.1
) for
k=0
), the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma A.2

(Extension lemma)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
p\in(1,\infty)
be such that
\alpha>1/p
.
For any
u_{0}\in\Psi B_{p,\theta+2/\alpha}^{\gamma+2-2/(\alpha p)}(\Omega)
, there exists
u\in\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,\infty)
such that
u(0)=u_{0}
and

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,\infty)}\leq N\|u_{0}\|_{\Psi B_{p,\theta+2/\alpha}^{\gamma+2-2/(\alpha p)}(\Omega)}\,,

(A.9)

where
N=N(d,\alpha,p,\gamma,\theta)
.

Proof.

Note that

\Psi B_{p,\theta+2/\alpha}^{\gamma+2-2/(\alpha p)}(\Omega)=\big(\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega),\Psi H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)\big)_{1/(\alpha p),p}

(see Lemma
2.16
),
where
\big(X_{0},X_{1}\big)_{1/(\alpha p),p}
is the real interpolation space introduced in
[
65
]
.
It was proved in
[
11
]
that for any interpolation couple
(X_{0},X_{1})
and any
u_{0}\in(X_{0},X_{1})_{1/(\alpha p),p}
, there exist
u\in L_{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X_{0})
and
f\in L_{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X_{1})
such that
u(0)=u_{0}
,

\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\alpha}\big(u(s)-u_{0}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,,

and

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X_{0})}+\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X_{1})}\lesssim_{\alpha,p}\|u_{0}\|_{(X_{0},X_{1})_{1/(\alpha p),p}}\,.

(A.10)

By putting
X_{0}:=\Psi H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega)
and
X_{1}:=\Psi H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, we obtain
u\in\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,\infty)
such that
u(0)\equiv u_{0}
and
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=f
.
Moreover, (
A.10
) yields (
A.9
).
∎

Appendix B
Auxiliary result for the fractional heat equation

Throughout this section, we fix constants
T
with
0<T<\infty
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and let
\Omega
be a bounded smooth domain.
For any
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega\big)
, there exists a unique function

u\in C^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega\big)\cap C\big([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\big)

such that
\mathrm{supp}(u)\subset(0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
and

\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u=\Delta u+f\quad\text{in}\quad(0,T]\times\Omega\quad;\quad u(t,x)=0\quad\text{if}\,\,\,\,t=0\,\,\,\,\text{or}\,\,\,\,x\in\partial\Omega\,.

Moreover, such a function
u
satisfies, for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u(t,x)|^{p-1}|D_{x}^{2}u(t,x)|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\,.

Proof.

The uniqueness of such
u
follows from the maximum principle (see
[
50
, Theorem 2]
).
Therefore, it remains to prove the existence of such
u
.

Recall that
\Omega
is a bounded smooth domain and
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega\big)
.
The following result follows from
[
22
, Theorem 2.10]
:

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
p\in(1,\infty)
, and
\theta\in(-p-1,-1)
, there exists a unique
u:(0,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
(
3.13
) holds for all
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T)\big)
, and

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k+2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\rho^{i}D_{x}^{i}u|^{p}\rho^{\theta-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\,.

(B.1)

Note that the solution spaces
\mathfrak{H}_{p,\theta,0}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
in
[
22
]
coincide with the space
\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p,\theta-p}^{\alpha,\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
defined in Definition
4.1
.(2) (see Lemma
4.5
.(1)).
Since
\Omega
is bounded, it follows that for any
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
k^{\prime}\geq k
,
1<p\leq p^{\prime}<\infty
, and
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\frac{\theta^{\prime}-d}{p^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\theta-d}{p}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k+2}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\rho^{i}D_{x}^{i}u|^{p}\rho^{\theta-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}+2}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\rho^{i}D_{x}^{i}u|^{p^{\prime}}\rho^{\theta^{\prime}-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}\,.

(B.2)

In addition,
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega)
, so that for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\theta\in(-p-1,-1)
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\rho^{k}D_{x}^{k}f|^{p}\rho^{\theta+2p-d}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\,.

(B.3)

Combining (
B.2
), (
B.3
), and
[
22
, Theorem 2.10]
,
we conclude that for
k=2
,
p\in(1,\infty)
, and
\theta=d-p
, the solutions satisfying (
B.1
) coincide.
Using this together with (
B.2
) and again invoking
[
22
, Theorem 2.10]
, we further deduce that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\in(1,\infty)
, and
\theta\in(d-p-1,d-1)
, the solutions satisfying (
B.1
) coincide.
We denote this unique solution by
Rf
.
Observe the following results:

-

Since (
B.1
) holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it is also obtained by (
2.25
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k}\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\rho^{i-1+d/p}D_{x}^{i}u(t,x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{k+1}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\rho^{i}D_{x}^{i}u|^{p}\rho^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

for any
p>d
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

-

Since
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega\big)
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
f(t,\cdot)\equiv 0
for all
0<t\leq\epsilon
.
By the uniqueness of
Rf
on the time interval
[0,\epsilon]
, we have
Rf(t,\cdot)=0
for all
0<t\leq\epsilon
.

Recall that, since
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega\big)
, we have
f(t,x)=I_{t}^{n}\big(\partial_{t}^{n}f\big)(t,x)
.
Denote
u_{n}:=R\big(\partial_{t}^{n}f\big)
.
One can observe that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
I^{n}_{t}u_{n}
is also solves (
3.13
).
Therefore, by the uniqueness of solutions to (
3.13
), we obtain that
u(t,x)=I_{t}^{n}u_{n}(t,x)
.
This implies that for any
m,\,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\partial_{t}^{n}D_{x}^{m}u=I_{t}\big(D_{x}^{m}u_{n+1}\big)
.
In addition, for any
p>d
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\rho(x)^{m-1+(d-1)/p}\partial_{t}^{n}D_{x}^{m}u(t,x)|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle T^{p-1}\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\rho(x)^{m-1+(d-1)/p}D_{x}^{m}u_{m+1}(t,x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\,.

It then follows that for any
t\in(0,T]
,
u(t,x)\rightarrow 0
as
x\in\partial\Omega
.
Moreover, by (
B.1
), we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p-1}|D_{x}^{2}u|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\rho^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\cdot\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|D_{x}^{2}u|^{p}\rho^{2p-2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\,.

∎

Proposition B.2
.

Let
0<\frac{1}{p}\leq\beta<\alpha\leq 1
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.
For any
u\in\mathcal{H}_{p}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
, the following estimates hold for all
0\leq s<t\leq T
and
A>0
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\big\|u(t,\cdot)-u(s,\cdot)\big\|_{B_{p}^{\gamma+2-2\beta/\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
\leq\,&N|t-s|^{\beta-1/p}A^{-\beta}\Big(\|u\|_{\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+A^{\alpha}\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u\|_{\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+A^{1/p}\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{B_{p}^{\gamma+2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)\,,\end{aligned}

(B.4)

and

\displaystyle\big\|u(t,\cdot)-u(s,\cdot)\big\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq N|t-s|^{\alpha-1/p}\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u\|_{\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}\,.

(B.5)

Here,
N=N(d,p,\gamma,\alpha,\beta)
.

Proof.

Considering
w:=(1-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}u
, it suffices to prove the case
\gamma=0
.
Without loss of generality, we assume
A=1
, since the dependence on
A
(
i.e.
, the exponent of
A
) can be deduced from the scaling in time.

Suppose for the moment that (
B.4
) holds for
\beta=0
and that (
B.5
) holds.
Note that

\displaystyle\big[L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big]_{\theta,p}=B_{p}^{\theta(2-2/(p\alpha))}\,.

(B.6)

Indeed,

\displaystyle L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\supset B_{p}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{and}\quad H_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\supset B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{if $p\leq 2$}\,,

\displaystyle L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset B_{p}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{and}\quad H_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{if $p\geq 2$}\,,

and

\big[L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),H_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big]_{\theta,p}=\big[B_{p}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big]_{\theta,p}=B_{p}^{\theta(2-2/(p\alpha))}\,.

Here we use the standard interpolation and embedding properties of
H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
B_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; see
[
66
, Sections 2.3 and 2.4]
.
Assuming (
B.4
) for
\beta=\frac{1}{p}
and (
B.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|u(t)-u(s)\|_{B_{p}^{\theta(2-2/(\alpha p))}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u(t)-u(s)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1-\theta}\|u(t)-u(s)\|_{B_{p}^{2-2/(\alpha p)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\theta}

\displaystyle\lesssim(t-s)^{(1-\theta)(\alpha-1/p)}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}\,,

where the first inequality follows from (
B.6
) together with
[
65
, Theorem 1.3.3.(g)]
.
By taking
\theta=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha-1/p}\in(0,1)
, we obtain that (
B.4
) also holds for
\frac{1}{p}<\beta<\alpha
.
Therefore, it suffices to prove (
B.4
) for
\beta=0
and (
B.5
).

Proof of (
B.4
) for
\beta=\frac{1}{p}
:

In this step, we employ results from
[
59
,
70
,
71
]
.
For this, we note that for any positive multiplier
m\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
satisfying
|D_{\xi}^{k}m(\xi)|\lesssim_{k}|\xi|^{k}m(\xi)
, the operator
f\mapsto\big(m(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)\big)^{\vee}
is
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial on
L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{R}
-angle
0
(see
[
23
, Example 10.1.3, Theorem G.5.2]
).

Consider the equation

\partial_{t}^{\alpha}v=\Delta v-v\quad\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad;\quad v(0,\cdot)=u(0,\cdot)\in B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,,

where
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}v
is understood in the sense of (
4.3
).
By
[
59
, Theorem 3.1]
, there exists a solution
v\in C\big([0,\infty);B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
such that

\displaystyle\|v\|_{C([0,\infty);B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\lesssim\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p}^{\alpha,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}\,.

(B.7)

Consider the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}w=\Delta w-w+\big(\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u-\Delta u+u\big)1_{(0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad;\quad w(0,\cdot)=0\,,

in the sense of (
4.3
).
By combining
[
70
, Theorem 3.1.1 and Remark 3.1.1.(v)]
and
[
71
, Theorem 3.6]
, we obtain the existence of a solution
w\in C\big([0,\infty);B_{p}^{2-2\beta/\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\big\|w\big\|_{C([0,\infty);B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\lesssim\big\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u-\Delta u+u\big\|_{L_{p}\left((0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}\lesssim\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p}^{\alpha,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}\,.\end{aligned}

(B.8)

The proof will be complete once we show that
u=v+w
.
Note that
u\in L_{p}\big((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
and
v+w\in C\big([0,T];B_{p}^{2-2/(p\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)\subset L_{p}\big((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
.
Put
\widetilde{u}=(1-\Delta)^{-1}\big(u-(v+w)\big)\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
.
Since

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}\widetilde{u}=\Delta\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{u}\quad\text{in}\quad(0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad;\quad\widetilde{u}(0,\,\cdot\,)\equiv 0

(B.9)

in the sense of (
4.3
), we obtain that
\widetilde{u}\in\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p}^{\alpha,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
(see Lemma
4.4
).
As established in
[
13
, Theorem 2.4]
, the solution to equation (
B.9
) in
\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p}^{\alpha,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
is unique.
(Note that
\mathbb{H}^{\alpha,2}_{p,0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{T})
in
[
13
]
coincides with
\mathring{\mathcal{H}}_{p}^{\alpha,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
by the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.5
.(1).)
Therefore
\widetilde{u}(t)\equiv 0
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
Since
u(t)
is a
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-valued continuous function for
t\in[0,T]
(see the result in Step 1), and
u=v+w
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, we conclude that
u(t,\cdot)\equiv v(t,\cdot)+w(t,\cdot)
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
Combining (
B.7
) and (
B.8
), we complete the proof.

Proof of (
B.5
):

When
\alpha=1
, this estimate follows from a direct calculation.
It remains to prove the case
0<\alpha<1
.
It follows from (
4.3
) and the Minkowski inequality that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|u(t)-u(s)\|_{p}\\
\lesssim_{\alpha,p}&\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u\|_{L_{p}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg(\int_{s}^{t}(t-r)^{-(1-\alpha)p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{s}\Big|(s-r)^{-1+\alpha}-(t-r)^{-1+\alpha}\Big|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}r\bigg)^{1/p^{\prime}}\,,\end{aligned}

(B.10)

where
p^{\prime}:=\frac{p}{p-1}
.
Since
(1-\alpha)p^{\prime}<1
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{s}^{t}(t-r)^{-(1-\alpha)p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}r\bigg)^{1/p^{\prime}}=N(\alpha,p)(t-s)^{\alpha-1/p}\,.

(B.11)

For the last term in (
B.10
), we note that if
s>0
, then for any
r,\,A>0
,

r^{-s}-(A+r)^{-s}\lesssim_{s}\left(\int_{0}^{A}(r+\ell)^{-s-1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell\right)\wedge r^{-s}\lesssim_{s}(A\wedge r)r^{-1-s}\simeq_{s}\frac{Ar^{-s}}{A+r}\,.

This implies that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}\Big|r^{-1+\alpha}-(t-s+r)^{-1+\alpha}\Big|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}r\lesssim(t-s)^{p^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{s}\frac{r^{-(1-\alpha)p^{\prime}}}{(t-s+r)^{p^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}r\,.

(B.12)

Since
(1-\alpha)p^{\prime}<1
and
(2-\alpha)p^{\prime}>1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{-(1-\alpha)p^{\prime}}}{(t-s+r)^{p^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}r=N(\alpha,p)(t-s)^{1-(2-\alpha)p^{\prime}}\,.

(B.13)

Combining (
B.10
)-(
B.13
), we obtain (
B.4
) for
\beta=\frac{1}{p}
.
∎
